\operatorname{I}(K)=\operatorname{I}(L)\cap K
.
The following is [ADH, 14.2.5]:

Lemma I.2.9
.

If
K
is
H
-asymptotic, has asymptotic integration, and is
1
-linearly newtonian, then it is
\operatorname{d}
-valued and
\der\mathcal{O}=\operatorname{I}(K)=(1+\smallo)^{\dagger}
.

We now turn our attention to the condition
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
.
If
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, then also
\operatorname{I}(K^{\phi})\subseteq(K^{\phi})^{\dagger}
for
\phi\in K^{\times}
, where

(K^{\phi})^{\dagger}\ :=\ \{\phi^{-1}f^{\prime}/f:\,f\in K^{\times}\}\ =\ \phi^{-1}K^{\dagger}.

By [ADH, Section 9.5 and 10.4.3]:

Lemma I.2.10
.

Let
K
be of
H
-type. If
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued, or pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued without a gap, then
K
has an immediate henselian asymptotic extension
L
with
\operatorname{I}(L)\subseteq L^{\dagger}
.

Corollary I.2.11
.

Suppose
K
has asymptotic integration. Let
L
be an asymptotic field extension of
K
such that
L^{\times}=K^{\times}C_{L}^{\times}(1+\smallo_{L})
.
Then
L^{\dagger}=K^{\dagger}+(1+\smallo_{L})^{\dagger}
, and
if
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, then
L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{\times}
, and
take
b\in K^{\times}
,
c\in C_{L}^{\times}
,
g\in\smallo_{L}

with
f=bc(1+g)
; then
f^{\dagger}=b^{\dagger}+(1+g)^{\dagger}
, showing
L^{\dagger}=K^{\dagger}+(1+\smallo_{L})^{\dagger}
.
Next, suppose
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, let

b
,
c
,
f
,
g
be as before, and assume
a:=f^{\dagger}\in K
;
then

a-b^{\dagger}\in(1+\smallo_{L})^{\dagger}\cap K\ \subseteq\ \operatorname{I}(L)\cap K\ =\ \operatorname{I}(K)\ \subseteq\ K^{\dagger}

and hence
a\in K^{\dagger}
. This shows
L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
.
∎

Two cases where the assumption on
L
in Corollary
I.2.11
is satisfied: (1)
L
is an immediate asymptotic field extension of
K
, because then
L^{\times}=K^{\times}({1+\smallo_{L}})
; and (2)

L
is a
\operatorname{d}
-valued field extension of
K
with
\Gamma=\Gamma_{L}
.

If
F
is a henselian valued field
of residue characteristic
0
, then clearly the subgroup
1+\smallo_{F}
of
F^{\times}
is divisible.
Hence, if
K
and
L
are as in Corollary
I.2.11
and in addition
K^{\dagger}
is divisible and
L
is henselian, then
L^{\dagger}
is divisible.

Example I.2.12
.

Let
C
be a field of characteristic
0
and
Q
be a subgroup of
{\mathbb{Q}}
with
1\in Q
. The Hahn field
C(\!(t^{Q})\!)=C[[x^{Q}]]
, with
x=t^{-1}
, is given the natural derivation with
c^{\prime}=0
for all
c\in C
and
x^{\prime}=1
: this derivation is defined by

\bigg(\sum_{q\in Q}c_{q}x^{q}\bigg)^{\prime}\ :=\ \sum_{q\in Q}qc_{q}x^{q-1}\qquad\text{(all $c_{q}\in C$)}.

Then
C(\!(t^{Q})\!)
has constant field
C
, and is
\operatorname{d}
-valued of
H
-type. Thus
K:=C(\!(t^{Q})\!)
satisfies
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}

by Lemma
I.2.10
. Hence by Lemma
I.2.8
,

\operatorname{I}(K)\ =\ (1+\smallo)^{\dagger}\ =\ \big\{f\in K:\,f\prec x^{\dagger}=t\big\}\ =\ \smallo\,t.

It follows easily that

K^{\dagger}=Qt\oplus\operatorname{I}(K)
(internal direct sum of subgroups of
K^{\dagger}
) and
thus
(K^{t})^{\dagger}=Q\oplus\smallo\subseteq\mathcal{O}
. In particular, if
Q={\mathbb{Z}}
(so
K=C(\!(t)\!)
), then
(K^{t})^{\dagger}={\mathbb{Z}}\oplus tC[[t]]
. Moreover, if
L:=\operatorname{P}(C)\subseteq C(\!(t^{{\mathbb{Q}}})\!)
is the differential field of Puiseux series over
C
, then

(L^{t})^{\dagger}={\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\smallo_{L}
.

The real closed case

In this subsection
H
is a real closed asymptotic field whose valuation ring
\mathcal{O}
is convex with respect to the ordering of
H
. (In later use
H
is often a Hardy field, which is why we use the letter
H
here.) The valuation ring of the asymptotic field extension
K=H[\imag]
of
H
is then
\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathcal{O}+\mathcal{O}\imag
, from which we obtain
\operatorname{I}(K)=\operatorname{I}(H)\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag
. Let

S\ :=\ \big\{y\in K:\,|y|=1\big\},\qquad W\ :=\ \big\{\!\operatorname{wr}(a,b):\,a,b\in H,\ a^{2}+b^{2}=1\big\},

so
S
is a subgroup of
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
with
S^{\dagger}=W\imag
and

K^{\dagger}=H^{\dagger}\oplus W\imag
by Lemma
I.2.4
.
Since
\der\mathcal{O}\subseteq\operatorname{I}(H)
,
we have
W\subseteq\operatorname{I}(H)
, and thus:
W=\operatorname{I}(H)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \operatorname{I}(H)\imag\subseteq K^{\dagger}
.

Lemma I.2.13
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
;

(ii)

W=\operatorname{I}(H)\subseteq H^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Assume (i).
Then
\operatorname{I}(H)\,\imag\subseteq\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, so
W=\operatorname{I}(H)
by the equivalence preceding the lemma. Also

\operatorname{I}(H)\subseteq\operatorname{I}(K)
and

K^{\dagger}\cap H=H^{\dagger}
(by Lemma
I.2.4
), hence
\operatorname{I}(H)\subseteq H^{\dagger}
, so
(ii) holds.
For the converse, assume (ii). Then

\operatorname{I}(K)\ =\ \operatorname{I}(H)\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag\ \subseteq\ H^{\dagger}\oplus W\imag\ =\ K^{\dagger}.\qed

Applying now Lemma
I.2.9
we obtain:

Corollary I.2.14
.

If
H
is
H
-asymptotic and has asymptotic integration, and
K
is
1
-linearly newtonian, then
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued and
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
; in particular,
W=\operatorname{I}(H)
.

Corollary I.2.15
.

Suppose
H
has asymptotic integration and
W=\operatorname{I}(H)
.
Let
F
be a real closed asymptotic extension of
H
whose valuation ring is convex.
Then

F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K\ =\ (F^{\dagger}\cap H)\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag.

If in addition
H^{\dagger}=H
, then
F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K=H\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag=K^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We have

F^{\dagger}\cap H\subseteq F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{I}(H)\imag=W\imag\subseteq K^{\dagger}\cap H\imag\subseteq F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K,

so
(F^{\dagger}\cap H)\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag\subseteq F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K
. For the reverse inclusion,

F[\imag]^{\dagger}=F^{\dagger}\oplus W_{F}\imag
, with

W_{F}\ :=\ \big\{\!\operatorname{wr}(a,b):\,a,b\in F,\ a^{2}+b^{2}=1\big\}\ \subseteq\ \operatorname{I}(F),

hence

\displaystyle F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K

\displaystyle\,=\,(F^{\dagger}\cap H)\oplus(W_{F}\cap H)\imag

\displaystyle\,\subseteq\,(F^{\dagger}\cap H)\oplus\big(\!\operatorname{I}(F)\cap H\big)\imag\,=\,(F^{\dagger}\cap H)\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag,

using
\operatorname{I}(F)\cap H=\operatorname{I}(H)
, a consequence of
H
having asymptotic integration.
If
{H^{\dagger}=H}
then clearly
F^{\dagger}\cap H=H
, hence
F[\imag]^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
.
∎

Trigonometric closure

In this subsection
H
is a real closed
H
-field. Let
\mathcal{O}
be its valuation ring and
\smallo
the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}
.
The algebraic closure
K=H[\imag]
of
H
is a
\operatorname{d}
-valued
H
-asymptotic extension with valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathcal{O}+\mathcal{O}\imag
.
We have the “complex conjugation” automorphism
z=a+b\imag\mapsto\overline{z}=a-b\imag
(
a,b\in H
) of the valued differential field
K
. For such
z
,
a
,
b
we have

|z|\ =\ \sqrt{z\overline{z}}\ =\ \sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}\ \in\ H^{\geqslant}.

Lemma I.2.16
.

Suppose
\theta\in H
and
\theta^{\prime}\imag\in K^{\dagger}
. Then
\theta^{\prime}\in\der\smallo
, and there is a unique
y\sim 1
in
K
such that
y^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
. For this
y
we have
|y|=1
, so
y^{-1}=\overline{y}
.

Proof.

From
\theta^{\prime}\imag\in K^{\dagger}
we get
\theta^{\prime}\in W\subseteq\operatorname{I}(H)
, so
\theta\preccurlyeq 1
, hence

\theta^{\prime}\in\der\mathcal{O}=\der\smallo
. Let
z\in K^{\times}
and
z^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
. Then
\operatorname{Re}z^{\dagger}=0
, so by Corollaries
I.2.5
and
I.2.7
we have
z=cy
with
c\in C_{K}^{\times}
and
y\in S\cap(1+\smallo_{K})
where
S=\{a\in K:\ |a|=1\}
. Hence
y\sim 1
,
|y|=1
, and
y^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
. If also
y_{1}\in K
and
y_{1}\sim 1
,
y_{1}^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
, then
y_{1}=c_{1}y
with
c_{1}\in C_{K}^{\times}
, so
c_{1}=1
in view of
y\sim y_{1}
.
∎

By [ADH, 10.4.3], if
y
in an
H
-asymptotic extension
L
of
K
satisfies

y\sim 1
and
{y^{\dagger}\in\der\smallo_{K}}
, then the asymptotic field
K(y)\subseteq L
is an immediate extension of
K
, and so is any algebraic asymptotic extension of
K(y)
.

Call
H

trigonometrically closed
if
for all
\theta\prec 1
in
H
there is a (necessarily unique)
y\in K
such that
y\sim 1
and
y^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
. (By convention “trigonometrically closed” includes “real closed”.) For such
\theta
and
y
we think of

y
as
\operatorname{e}^{\imag\theta}
and accordingly of the elements
\frac{y+\overline{y}}{2}=\frac{y+y^{-1}}{2}
and
\frac{y-\overline{y}}{2\imag}=\frac{y-y^{-1}}{2\imag}
of
H
as
\cos\theta
and
\sin\theta
; this explains the terminology. By Lemma
I.2.16
the restrictions
\theta\prec 1
and
y\sim 1
are harmless. Our aim in this subsection is to construct a canonical trigonometric closure
of
H
.

Our interest in this notion comes from the condition
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, which appears as a natural hypothesis
at many points in Part
IV
, especially in Section
IV.4
).
Note that if
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, then
H
is trigonometrically closed. As a partial converse, if
\operatorname{I}(H)\subseteq H^{\dagger}\cap\der H
and
H
is trigonometrically closed, then
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
; this is an
easy consequence of
\operatorname{I}(K)=\operatorname{I}(H)+\operatorname{I}(H)\imag
. Thus for Liouville closed
H
we have:

H\text{ is trigonometrically closed}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}.

Note also that for trigonometrically closed
H
there is no
y
in any
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
such that

y\notin K
,
y\sim 1
, and
y^{\dagger}\in(\der\smallo)\imag
.
If
H
is Schwarz closed, then
H
is trigonometrically closed by the next lemma:

Lemma I.2.17
.

Suppose
H
is Liouville closed and
\omega(H)
is downward closed. Then
H
is trigonometrically closed.

Proof.

Let
0\neq\theta\prec 1
in
H
. By Lemma
I.2.16
it suffices to show that then
{\theta^{\prime}\imag\in K^{\dagger}}
.
Note that
h:=\theta^{\prime}\in\operatorname{I}(H)^{\neq}
; we arrange
h>0
. Now

f\ :=\ \omega(-h^{\dagger})+4h^{2}\ =\ \sigma(2h),\qquad 2h\in H^{>}\cap\operatorname{I}(H),

hence
2h\in H^{>}\setminus\Upgamma(H)

by [ADH, 11.8.19]. So
f\in\omega(H)^{\downarrow}=\omega(H)
by [ADH, 11.8.31], and thus

\dim_{C_{H}}\ker 4\der^{2}+f\geqslant 1
by [ADH, p. 258].
Put
A:=\der^{2}-h^{\dagger}\der+h^{2}\in H[\der]
.
The isomorphism
y\mapsto y\sqrt{h}\colon\ker({4\der^{2}+f})\to\ker A
of
C_{H}
-linear spaces [ADH, 5.1.13] then yields an element of
\ker^{\neq}A
that for suggestiveness we denote by
\cos\theta
.
Put
\sin\theta:=-(\cos\theta)^{\prime}/h
.
Then

\displaystyle(\sin\theta)^{\prime}\

\displaystyle=\ -(\cos\theta)^{\prime\prime}/h+(\cos\theta)^{\prime}h^{\dagger}/h

\displaystyle=\ \big({-h^{\dagger}(\cos\theta)^{\prime}+h^{2}\cos\theta}\big)/h+(\cos\theta)^{\prime}h^{\dagger}/h\ =\ h\cos\theta

and thus
y^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
for
y:=\cos\theta+\imag\sin\theta\in K^{\times}
.
∎

If
H
is
H
-closed, then
H
is Schwarz closed by [ADH, 14.2.20], and thus trigonometrically closed. Using also Lemma
I.2.13
and remarks preceding it this yields:

Corollary I.2.18
.

If
H
is
H
-closed, then
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}=H\oplus\operatorname{I}(H)\imag
.

Suppose now that
H
is
not
trigonometrically closed; so we have
\theta\prec 1
in
H
with
\theta^{\prime}\imag\notin K^{\dagger}
.
Then [ADH, 10.4.3] provides an immediate asymptotic extension
K(y)
of
K
with
y\sim 1
and

y^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
. To simplify notation and for suggestiveness we set

\cos\theta\ :=\ \frac{y+y^{-1}}{2},\qquad\sin\theta\ :=\ \frac{y-y^{-1}}{2\imag},

so
y=\cos\theta+\imag\sin\theta
and

(\cos\theta)^{2}+(\sin\theta)^{2}=1
. Moreover
(\cos\theta)^{\prime}=-\theta^{\prime}\sin\theta
and
(\sin\theta)^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}\cos\theta
. It follows that

H^{+}:=H(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
is a differential subfield of
K(y)
with
K(y)=H^{+}[\imag]
, and thus
H^{+}
, as a valued differential
subfield of
H(y)
, is an asymptotic extension of
H
.

Lemma I.2.19
.

H^{+}
is an immediate extension of
H
.

Proof.

Since
(y^{-1})^{\dagger}=-\theta^{\prime}\imag
, the uniqueness property stated in [ADH, 10.4.3] allows us to extend the complex conjugation automorphism
of
K
(which is the identity on
H
and sends
\imag
to
-\imag
) to
an automorphism
\sigma
of the valued differential field
K(y)
such that
\sigma(y)=y^{-1}
.
Then
\sigma(\cos\theta)=\cos\theta
and
\sigma(\sin\theta)=\sin\theta
, so
H^{+}=\text{Fix}(\sigma)
.
Let
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
be the residue field of
H
; so
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}[\operatorname{res}\imag]
is the residue field of
K
and of its immediate extension
K(y)
.
Now
\sigma(\mathcal{O}_{K(y)})=\mathcal{O}_{K(y)}
, so
\sigma
induces an automorphism of this residue field
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}[\operatorname{res}\imag]

which is the identity on
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
and sends
\operatorname{res}\imag
to
-\operatorname{res}\imag
. Hence
\operatorname{res}\imag
does not lie in the residue field of
H^{+}
, so this residue field is just
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
.
∎

Equip
H^{+}
with the unique field ordering making it an ordered field extension of
H
in which
\mathcal{O}_{H^{+}}
is convex; see
[ADH, 10.5.8]. Then
H^{+}
is an
H
-field, and its real closure is an immediate real closed
H
-field extension of
H
.

Lemma I.2.20
.

The
H
-field
H^{+}
embeds uniquely over
H
into any trigonometrically
closed
H
-field extension of
H
.

Proof.

Let
H^{*}
be a trigonometrically closed
H
-field extension of
H
. Take the unique
z\sim 1
in
H^{*}
such that
z^{\dagger}=\theta^{\prime}\imag
. Then any
H
-field embedding
H^{+}\to H^{*}
over
H
extends to a valued differential field embedding
H^{+}[\imag]=K(y)\to H^{*}[\imag]
sending
\imag\in K
to
\imag\in H^{*}[\imag]
, and this extension
must send
y
to
z
. Hence there is at most one
H
-field embedding
H^{+}\to H^{*}
over
H
.
For the existence of such an embedding, the uniqueness properties from [ADH, 10.4.3] yield a valued differential field embedding
K(y)\to H^{*}[\imag]
over
H
sending
\imag\in K
to
\imag\in H^{*}[\imag]
and
y
to
z
. This embedding maps
H^{+}
into
H^{*}
. The uniqueness property of the ordering
on
H^{+}
shows that this embedding restricts to an
H
-field embedding
H^{+}\to H^{*}
.
∎

By iterating the extension step that leads from
H
to
H^{+}
, alternating it with taking real closures, and taking unions at
limit stages we obtain:

Proposition I.2.21
.

H
has a trigonometrically closed
H
-field extension
H^{\operatorname{trig}}

that embeds uniquely over
H
into any trigonometrically closed
H
-field extension of
H
.

This is an easy consequence of Lemma
I.2.20
. Note that the universal property stated in Proposition
I.2.21
determines
H^{\operatorname{trig}}
up-to-unique-isomorphism of
H
-fields over
H
. We refer to such
H^{\operatorname{trig}}
as the
trigonometric closure
of
H
. Note that
H^{\operatorname{trig}}
is an immediate extension of
H
, by Lemma
I.2.19
,
and that
H^{\operatorname{trig}}[\imag]
is a Liouville extension of
K
and thus of
H
.

A
trigonometric extension
of
H
is a real closed
H
-field extension
E
of
H
such that for all
a\in E
there are
real closed
H
-subfields
H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{n}
of
E
such that

(1)

H_{0}=H
and
a\in H_{n}
;

(2)

for
j=0,\dots,n-1
there are
\theta_{j}\in H_{j}
and
y_{j}\in H_{j+1}[\imag]\subseteq E[\imag]
such that
y_{j}\sim 1
,
\theta_{j}^{\prime}\imag=y_{j}^{\dagger}
, and
H_{j+1}[\imag]
is algebraic over
H_{j}[\imag](y_{j})
.

If
E
is a trigonometric extension of
H
, then
E
is an immediate extension of
H
and
E[\imag]
is an immediate Liouville extension of
K
and thus of
H
.
The next lemma states some further easy consequences of the definition above:

Lemma I.2.22
.

If
E
is a trigonometric extension of
H
, then
E
is a trigonometric extension of any
real closed
H
-subfield
F\supseteq H
of
E
.
If
H
is trigonometrically closed, then
H
has no proper trigonometric extension.

Induction on
m
shows that if
E
is a trigonometric extension of
H
, then for any
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in E
there are real closed
H
-subfields

H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq H_{n}
of
E
such that
H_{0}=H
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in H_{n}
and (2) above holds. This helps in proving:

Corollary I.2.23
.

A trigonometric extension of a trigonometric extension of
H
is a trigonometric extension of
H
, and
H^{\operatorname{trig}}
is a trigonometric extension of
H
.

Asymptotic fields of Hardy type

Let
(\Gamma,\psi)
be an asymptotic couple,
\Psi:=\psi(\Gamma^{\neq})
, and let
\gamma
,
\delta
range over
\Gamma
.
Recall that

[\gamma]

denotes the archimedean class of
\gamma
[ADH, 2.4].
Following
[
24
, Section 3]
we say that
(\Gamma,\psi)
is of
Hardy type
if
for all
\gamma,\delta\neq 0
we have

[\gamma]\leqslant[\delta]\Longleftrightarrow\psi(\gamma)\geqslant\psi(\delta)
.
Note that then
(\Gamma,\psi)
is of
H
-type, and
\psi
induces an order-reversing bijection
[\Gamma^{\neq}]\to\Psi
.
If
\Gamma
is archimedean, then
(\Gamma,\psi)
is of Hardy type. If
(\Gamma,\psi)
is of Hardy type, then so is

(\Gamma,\psi+\delta)
for each
\delta
.
We also say that an asymptotic field is of Hardy type if its asymptotic couple is. Every asymptotic subfield
and every compositional conjugate of
an asymptotic field of Hardy type is also of Hardy type.
Moreover, every Hardy field is of Hardy type [ADH, 9.1.11].
Let now
\Delta
be a convex subgroup of
\Gamma
. Note that

\Delta
contains the archimedean class
[\delta]
of each
\delta\in\Delta
. Hence, if

\delta\in\Delta^{\neq}
and
\gamma\notin\Delta
, then
[\delta]<[\gamma]
and thus:

Lemma I.2.24
.

If
(\Gamma,\psi)
is of Hardy type and
\gamma\notin\Delta
,
\delta\in\Delta^{\neq}
, then

\psi(\gamma)<\psi(\delta)
.

Corollary I.2.25
.

Suppose
(\Gamma,\psi)
is of Hardy type with small derivation,
\gamma,\delta\neq 0
,
\psi(\delta)\leqslant 0
, and
[\gamma^{\prime}]>[\delta]
. Then
\psi(\gamma)<\psi(\delta)
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta
be the smallest convex subgroup of
\Gamma
with
\delta\in\Delta
; then
\gamma^{\prime}\notin\Delta
, and
\psi(\delta)\in\Delta

by [ADH, 9.2.10(iv)]. Thus
\gamma\notin\Delta
by [ADH, 9.2.25].
∎

In
[
1
, Section 7]
we say that an
H
-field
H
is
closed under powers
if for all
c\in C
and
f\in H^{\times}
there is a
y\in H^{\times}

with
y^{\dagger}=cf^{\dagger}
. (Think of
y
as
f^{c}
.) Thus if
H
is Liouville closed, then
H
is closed under powers.

In the rest of this subsection we let
H
be an
H
-field closed under powers, with asymptotic couple
(\Gamma,\psi)
and constant field
C
.

We recall some basic facts from
[
1
, Section 7]
.
First, we can make the value group
\Gamma
into
an ordered vector space over the constant field
C
:

Lemma I.2.26
.

For all
c\in C
and
\gamma=vf
with
f\in H^{\times}
and each
y\in H^{\times}

with
y^{\dagger}=cf^{\dagger}
, the element
vy\in\Gamma
only depends on

(c,\gamma)

(
not on the choice of
f
and
y
)
, and is denoted by
c\cdot\gamma
. The scalar multiplication

(c,\gamma)\mapsto c\cdot\gamma\colon C\times\Gamma\to\Gamma

makes
\Gamma
into an ordered vector space over the ordered field
C
.

Let
G
be an ordered vector space over the ordered field
C
. From [ADH, 2.4] recall that
the
C
-archimedean class of
a\in G
is defined
as

[a]_{C}:=\big\{b\in G:\,\textstyle\frac{1}{c}|a|\leqslant|b|\leqslant c|a|\text{ for some }c\in C^{>}\big\}.

Thus if
C={\mathbb{Q}}
, then
[a]_{\mathbb{Q}}
is just the archimedean class
[a]
of
a\in G
.
Moreover, if
C^{*}
is an ordered subfield of
C
, then
[a]_{C^{*}}\subseteq[a]_{C}
for each
a\in G
,
with equality if
C^{*}
is cofinal in
C
.
Hence if

C
is archimedean, then
[a]=[a]_{C}
for all
a\in G
.
Put
[G]_{C}:=\bigl\{[a]_{C}:a\in G\bigr\}
and linearly order
[G]_{C}
by

[a]_{C}<[b]_{C}\quad:\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{$[a]_{C}\neq[b]_{C}$ and $|a|<|b|$.}

Thus
[G]_{C}
has smallest element
[0]_{C}=\{0\}
. We also set
[G^{\neq}]_{C}:=[G]_{C}\setminus\big\{[0]_{C}\big\}
.
From
[
1
, Proposition 7.5]
we have:

Proposition I.2.27
.

For all
\gamma,\delta\neq 0
we have

[\gamma]_{C}\leqslant[\delta]_{C}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\psi(\gamma)\geqslant\psi(\delta).

Hence
\psi
induces an order-reversing bijection
[\Gamma^{\neq}]_{C}\to\Psi=\psi(\Gamma^{\neq})
.

Proposition
I.2.27
yields:

Corollary I.2.28
.

H
is of Hardy type
\Longleftrightarrow

[\gamma]=[\gamma]_{C}
for all
\gamma
.
Hence if
C
is archimedean, then
H
is of Hardy type; if
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
, then the converse also holds.

I.3.
\uplambda
-freeness and
\upomega
-freeness

This section contains preservation results for the important properties of
\uplambda
-freeness
and
\upomega
-freeness
from [ADH]. Let
K
be an ungrounded
H
-asymptotic field such that
{\Gamma\neq\{0\}}
, and
as in [ADH, 11.5], fix a logarithmic sequence
(\ell_{\rho})
for
K
and define the pc-sequences
(\uplambda_{\rho})=(-\ell_{\rho}^{\dagger\dagger})
and
(\upomega_{\rho})=\big(\omega(\uplambda_{\rho})\big)
in
K
, where
\omega(z):=-2z^{\prime}-z^{2}
.
Recall that
K
is
\uplambda
-free
iff
(\uplambda_{\rho})
does not have a pseudolimit in
K
, and
K
is
\upomega
-free
iff
(\upomega_{\rho})
does not have a pseudolimit in
K
.
If
K
is
\upomega
-free, then
K
is
\uplambda
-free.
We refer to [ADH, 11.6, 11.7] for this and other basic facts about
\uplambda
-freeness and
\upomega
-freeness used below. As in [ADH],
L
being
\uplambda
-free or
\upomega
-free includes
L
being an ungrounded
H
-asymptotic field with
\Gamma_{L}\neq\{0\}
.

Preserving
\uplambda
-freeness and
\upomega
-freeness

In this subsection
K
is an ungrounded
H
-asymptotic field with
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
, and
(\ell_{\rho})
,
(\uplambda_{\rho})
,

(\upomega_{\rho})
are as above.

If
K
has a
\uplambda
-free
H
-asymptotic field extension
L
such that
\Gamma^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{L}^{<}
, then
K
is

\uplambda
-free, and similarly with “
\upomega
-free” in place of “
\uplambda
-free” [ADH, remarks after 11.6.4, 11.7.19].
The property of
\upomega
-freeness is very robust; indeed, by [ADH, 13.6.1]:

Theorem I.3.1
.

If
K
is
\upomega
-free and
L
is a pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
, then
L
is
\upomega
-free and
\Gamma^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{L}^{<}
.

In contrast,
\uplambda
-freeness is more delicate: Theorem
I.3.1
fails with “
\uplambda
-free” in place of “
\upomega
-free”, as the next example shows.

Example I.3.2
.

The
H
-field
K={\mathbb{R}}\langle\upomega\rangle
from [ADH, 13.9.1] is
\uplambda
-free, but its
H
-field extension

L={\mathbb{R}}\langle\uplambda\rangle
is not, and this extension is
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic:
2\uplambda^{\prime}+\uplambda^{2}+\upomega=0
.

In the rest of this subsection we consider cases where parts of Theorem
I.3.1
do hold.
Recall from [ADH, 11.6.8] that if
K
is
\uplambda
-free, then
K
has (rational) asymptotic integration, and
K

is
\uplambda
-free iff its algebraic closure is
\uplambda
-free.
Moreover,
\uplambda
-freeness is preserved under adjunction of constants:

Proposition I.3.3
.

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
L=K(D)
is an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
with
D\supseteq C
a subfield of
C_{L}
.
Then
L
is
\uplambda
-free with
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma
.

We are going to deduce this from the next three lemmas.
Recall that
K
is pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued, by [ADH, 10.1.3]. Let
\operatorname{dv}(K)
be the
\operatorname{d}
-valued hull
of
K
(see [ADH, 10.3]).

Lemma I.3.4
.

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free. Then
L:=\operatorname{dv}(K)
is
\uplambda
-free and
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma
.

Proof.

The first statement is
[
13
, Theorem 10.2]
,
and the second statement follows from [ADH, 10.3.2(i)].
∎

If
L=K(D)
is a differential field extension of
K

with
D\supseteq C
a subfield of
C_{L}
, then
D=C_{L}
, and
K
and
D
are linearly disjoint over
C
[ADH, 4.6.20].
If
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued and
L=K(D)
is an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
with
D\supseteq C
a subfield of
C_{L}
,
then
L
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued and
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma
[ADH, 10.5.15].

Lemma I.3.5
.

Suppose
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued and
\uplambda
-free, and
L=K(D)
is an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
with
D\supseteq C
a subfield of
C_{L}
. Then
L
is
\uplambda
-free.

Proof.

First,
(\uplambda_{\rho})
is of transcendental type over
K
: otherwise, [ADH, 3.2.7] would give an algebraic extension of
K
that is not
\uplambda
-free. Next, our logarithmic sequence
(\ell_{\rho})
for
K
remains a logarithmic sequence for
L
.

Zorn and the
\forall\exists
-form of the
\uplambda
-freeness axiom [ADH, 1.6.1(ii)] reduce us to the case
D=C(d)
,
d\notin C
,
d
transcendental over
K
, so
L=K(d)
.
Suppose
L
is not
\uplambda
-free. Then
\uplambda_{\rho}\leadsto\uplambda\in L
, and such
\uplambda
is transcendental over
K
and gives an immediate extension
K(\uplambda)
of
K
by [ADH, 3.2.6].
Hence
L
is algebraic over
K(\uplambda)
, so

\operatorname{res}L
is algebraic over
\operatorname{res}K(\uplambda)=\operatorname{res}K\cong C
and thus
d
is algebraic over
C
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma I.3.6
.

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
L
is an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
, where
L=K(d)
with
d\in C_{L}
.
Then
L
is pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued.

Proof.

Let
L^{\operatorname{a}}
be an algebraic closure of the
H
-asymptotic field
L
, and let
K^{\operatorname{a}}
be the algebraic closure of
K
inside
L^{\operatorname{a}}
. Then
K^{\operatorname{a}}
is pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued by [ADH, 10.1.22]. Replacing
K
,
L
by
K^{\operatorname{a}}
,
K^{\operatorname{a}}(d)
we arrange that

K
is algebraically closed. We may assume
d\notin C
, so
d
is transcendental over
K
by [ADH, 4.1.1, 4.1.2].

Suppose first that
\operatorname{res}(d)\in\operatorname{res}(K)\subseteq\operatorname{res}(L)
, and take
b\in\mathcal{O}
such that

y:=b-d\prec 1
.
Then
b^{\prime}\notin\der\smallo
: otherwise
y^{\prime}=b^{\prime}=\delta^{\prime}
with
\delta\in\smallo
, so
y=\delta\in K
and hence
d\in K
,
a contradiction.
Also
vb^{\prime}\in(\Gamma^{>})^{\prime}
: otherwise
vb^{\prime}<(\Gamma^{>})^{\prime}
, by [ADH, 9.2.14], and
vb^{\prime}
would be a gap in
K
, contradicting

\uplambda
-freeness of
K
. Hence
L=K(y)
is pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued by [ADH, 10.2.4, 10.2.5(iii)] applied to
s:=b^{\prime}
.

If
\operatorname{res}(d)\notin\operatorname{res}(K)
, then
\operatorname{res}(d)
is transcendental over
\operatorname{res}(K)
by [ADH, 3.1.17], hence

\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma
by [ADH, 3.1.11], and so
L
has asymptotic integration and thus is pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued by [ADH, 10.1.3].
∎

Proof of Proposition
I.3.3
.

By Zorn we reduce to the case
L=K(d)
with
d\in C_{L}
.
Then
L
is pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued by Lemma
I.3.6
.
By Lemma
I.3.4
, the
\operatorname{d}
-valued hull
K_{1}:=\operatorname{dv}(K)
of
K
is
\uplambda
-free with
\Gamma_{K_{1}}=\Gamma
, and by the universal property
of
\operatorname{d}
-valued hulls we may arrange that
K_{1}
is a
\operatorname{d}
-valued subfield of
L_{1}:=\operatorname{dv}(L)
[ADH, 10.3.1].
The proof of [ADH, 10.3.1] gives
L_{1}=L(E)
where
E=C_{L_{1}}
, and so

L_{1}=K_{1}(E)
. Hence by Lemma
I.3.5
and the remarks preceding it,
L_{1}
is
\uplambda
-free with
\Gamma_{L_{1}}=\Gamma_{K_{1}}=\Gamma
.
Thus
L
is
\uplambda
-free with
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma
.
∎

Lemma I.3.7
.

Let
H
be a
\uplambda
-free real closed
H
-field. Then the trigonometric closure
H^{\operatorname{trig}}
of
H
is
\uplambda
-free.

Proof.

We show that
H^{+}
as in Lemma
I.2.19
is
\uplambda
-free. There
H^{+}[\imag]=K(y)
where
K
is the
H
-asymptotic extension
H[\imag]
of
H
and
y\sim 1
,
y^{\dagger}\notin K^{\dagger}
,
y^{\dagger}\in\imag\der\smallo_{H}
. Then
K
is
\uplambda
-free, so
K(y)
is
\uplambda
-free by
[
13
, Proposition 7.2]
,
hence
H^{+}
is
\uplambda
-free.
∎

In Example
I.3.2
we have a
\uplambda
-free
K
and an
H
-asymptotic extension
L
of
K
that is not
\uplambda
-free, with
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)=1
.
The next proposition shows that the second part of the conclusion of Theorem
I.3.1
nevertheless
holds for such
K
,
L
.

Proposition I.3.8
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

K
has rational asymptotic integration;

(ii)

for every
H
-asymptotic extension
L
of
K
with
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)\leqslant 1
we have that

\Gamma^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{L}^{<}
.

Proof.

For (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii), assume (i), and let
L
be an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
with
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)\leqslant 1
. Towards showing that
\Gamma^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{L}^{<}
we can arrange that
K
and
L
are algebraically closed. Suppose towards a contradiction that
\gamma\in\Gamma_{L}
and

\Gamma^{<}<\gamma<0
. Then
\Psi<\gamma^{\prime}<(\Gamma^{>})^{\prime}
, and so
\Gamma
is dense in

\Gamma+{\mathbb{Q}}\gamma^{\prime}
by [ADH, 2.4.16, 2.4.17], in particular,
\gamma\notin\Gamma+{\mathbb{Q}}\gamma^{\prime}
. Thus

\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linearly independent over
\Gamma
, which contradicts
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)\leqslant 1
by [ADH, 3.1.11].

As to (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i), we prove the contrapositive, so assume
K
does not have rational asymptotic integration.
We arrange again that
K
is algebraically closed.
Then
K
has a gap
vs
with
s\in K^{\times}
, and so [ADH, 10.2.1 and its proof] gives an
H
-asymptotic extension
K(y)
of
K
with
y^{\prime}=s
and
0<vy<\Gamma^{>}
.
∎

Recall from [ADH, 11.6] that Liouville closed
H
-fields are
\uplambda
-free.
To prove the next result we also use Gehret’s theorem
[
13
, Theorem 12.1(1)]

that an
H
-field
H
has up to isomorphism over
H
exactly one Liouville closure iff
H
is grounded or
\uplambda
-free. Here
isomorphism
means of course
isomorphism of
H
-fields
, and likewise with the embeddings referred to in the next result:

Proposition I.3.9
.

Let
H
be a grounded or
\uplambda
-free
H
-field. Then
H
has a trigonometrically closed
and Liouville closed
H
-field extension
H^{\operatorname{tl}}
that embeds over
H
into any trigonometrically closed
Liouville closed
H
-field extension of
H
.

Proof.

We build real closed
H
-fields
H_{0}\subseteq H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}\subseteq\cdots
as follows:
H_{0}
is a real closure of
H
, and, recursively,

H_{2n+1}
is a Liouville closure of
H_{2n}
, and
H_{2n+2}:=H_{2n+1}^{\operatorname{trig}}
is the trigonometric closure of
H_{2n+1}
.
Then
H^{*}:=\bigcup_{n}H_{n}
is a trigonometrically closed Liouville closed
H
-field extension of
H
.
Induction using Lemma
I.3.7
shows that all
H_{n}
with
n\geqslant 1
are
\uplambda
-free, and that

H_{2n}
has for all
n
up to isomorphism over
H
a unique Liouville closure. Given any trigonometrically closed Liouville closed
H
-field extension
E
of
H
we then use the embedding properties of
Liouville closure
and
trigonometric closure
to construct by a similar recursion embeddings
H_{n}\to E
that extend to an embedding
H^{*}\to E
over
H
.
∎

For
H
as in Proposition
I.3.9
, the
H^{*}
constructed in its proof is minimal: Let
{E\supseteq H}
be any trigonometrically closed Liouville closed
H
-subfield of
H^{*}
. Then induction on
n
yields
H_{n}\subseteq~E
for all
n
, so
E=H^{*}
.
It follows that any
H^{\operatorname{tl}}
as in Proposition
I.3.9
is isomorphic over
H
to
H^{*}
, and we refer to such

H^{\operatorname{tl}}
as a
trigonometric-Liouville closure
of
H
.
Here are some useful facts about
H^{\operatorname{tl}}
:

Corollary I.3.10
.

Let
H
be a
\uplambda
-free
H
-field. Then
C_{H^{\operatorname{tl}}}
is a real closure of
C_{H}
, the
H
-asymptotic extension
K^{\operatorname{tl}}:=H^{\operatorname{tl}}[\imag]
of
H^{\operatorname{tl}}

is a Liouville extension of
H
with
\operatorname{I}(K^{\operatorname{tl}})\subseteq(K^{\operatorname{tl}})^{\dagger}
, and
\Gamma_{H}^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{H^{\operatorname{tl}}}^{<}
. Moreover,

\text{$H$ is $\upomega$-free}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \text{$H^{\operatorname{tl}}$ is $\upomega$-free.}

Proof.

The construction of
H^{*}
in the proof of Proposition
I.3.9
gives that
C_{H^{*}}
is a real closure of
C_{H}
,
and that the
H
-asymptotic extension
K^{*}:=H^{*}[\imag]
of
H^{*}

is a Liouville extension of
H
with
\operatorname{I}(K^{*})\subseteq(K^{*})^{\dagger}
. Induction using Lemma
I.3.7
and Proposition
I.3.8
shows that
H_{n}
is
\uplambda
-free and
\Gamma_{H}^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{H_{n}}^{<}
, for all
n
, so
\Gamma_{H}^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{H^{*}}^{<}
.

The final equivalence follows from Theorem
I.3.1
and a remark preceding it.
∎

Proposition
I.3.8
and
[ADH, remarks after 11.6.4 and after 11.7.19] yield:

Corollary I.3.11
.

Suppose
K
has rational asymptotic integration, and let
L
be an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
with
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)\leqslant 1
.
If
L
is
\uplambda
-free, then so is
K
, and if
L
is
\upomega
-free, then so is
K
.

We also have a similar characterization of
\uplambda
-freeness:

Proposition I.3.12
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

K
is
\uplambda
-free;

(ii)

every
H
-asymptotic extension
L
of
K
with
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)\leqslant 1
has asymptotic integration.

Proof.

Assume
K
is
\uplambda
-free, and let
L
be an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
such that
\operatorname{trdeg}(L|K)\leqslant 1
.
By Proposition
I.3.8
,
\Gamma^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{L}^{<}
, so
L
is ungrounded.
Towards
a contradiction, suppose
vf
(
f\in L^{\times}
) is a gap in
L
.
Passing to algebraic closures we arrange that
K
and
L
are algebraically closed.
Set
\uplambda:=-f^{\dagger}
. Then for all active
a
in
L

we have
\uplambda+a^{\dagger}\prec a
by [ADH, 11.5.9] and hence
\uplambda_{\rho}\leadsto\uplambda
by [ADH, 11.5.6]. By
\uplambda
-freeness of
K

and [ADH, 3.2.6, 3.2.7], the valued field extension
K(\uplambda)\supseteq K
is immediate of transcendence degree
1
, so
L\supseteq K(\uplambda)
is algebraic and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{L}
. Hence
vf
is a gap in
K
, a contradiction. This shows (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii).

To show the contrapositive of (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i),
suppose
\uplambda\in K
is a pseudolimit of
(\uplambda_{\rho})
. If the algebraic closure
K^{\operatorname{a}}
of
K
does not have asymptotic integration,
then clearly (ii) fails. If
K^{\operatorname{a}}
has asymptotic integration,
then
-\uplambda
creates a gap over
K
by [ADH, 11.5.14] applied to
K^{\operatorname{a}}
in place of
K
, hence (ii) also fails.
∎

The next two lemmas include converses to Lemmas
I.3.4
and
I.3.5
.

Lemma I.3.13
.

Let
E
be a pre-
\operatorname{d}
-valued
H
-asymptotic field. Then:

(i)

if
E
is not
\uplambda
-free, then
\operatorname{dv}(E)
is not
\uplambda
-free;

(ii)

if
E
is not
\upomega
-free, then
\operatorname{dv}(E)
is not
\upomega
-free.

Proof.

This is clear if
E
has no rational asymptotic integration, because then
\operatorname{dv}(E)
has no rational asymptotic integration either, by [ADH, 10.3.2]. Assume
E
has rational asymptotic integration. Then
\operatorname{dv}(E)

is an immediate extension of
E
by [ADH, 10.3.2], and then (i) and (ii) follow from the characterizations of
\uplambda
-freeness and

\upomega
-freeness in terms of nonexistence of certain pseudolimits.
∎

Lemma I.3.14
.

Let
E
be a
\operatorname{d}
-valued
H
-asymptotic field and
F
an
H
-asymptotic extension of
E
such that

F=E(C_{F})
.
Then:

(i)

if
E
is not
\uplambda
-free, then
F
is not
\uplambda
-free;

(ii)

if
E
is not
\upomega
-free, then
F
is not
\upomega
-free.

Proof.

By [ADH, 10.5.15]
E
and
F
have the same value group. The rest of the proof is like that for the previous lemma, with
F
instead of
\operatorname{dv}(E)
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection
K
is in addition a pre-
H
-field and
L
a pre-
H
-field extension of
K
.

The following is shown in the proof of
[
13
, Lemma 12.5]
:

Proposition I.3.15

(Gehret)
.

Suppose
K
is a
\uplambda
-free
H
-field and
L
is a Liouville
H
-field extension of
K
.
Then
L
is
\uplambda
-free and
\Gamma^{<}
is cofinal in
\Gamma_{L}^{<}
.

Example I.3.16
.

Let
K={\mathbb{R}}\langle\upomega\rangle
be the
\uplambda
-free but non-
\upomega
-free
H
-field from [ADH, 13.9.1]. Then

K
has a unique Liouville closure
L
, up to isomorphism over
K
, by
[
13
, Theorem 12.1(1)]
.
By Proposition
I.3.15
,
L

is not
\upomega
-free; another proof of this fact is in
[
2
]
. By [ADH, 13.9.5] we can take
here
K
to be a Hardy field, and then
L
is isomorphic over
K
to a Hardy field
extension of
K
[ADH, 10.6.11].

Applying Corollary
I.3.10
to
H:={\mathbb{R}}\langle\upomega\rangle
yields a Liouville closed
H
-field
H^{\operatorname{tl}}
that is not

\upomega
-free but does satisfy
\operatorname{I}(K^{\operatorname{tl}})\subseteq(K^{\operatorname{tl}})^{\dagger}
for
K^{\operatorname{tl}}:=H^{\operatorname{tl}}[\imag]
.

Lemma I.3.17
.

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free,
\uplambda\in\Uplambda(L)^{\downarrow}
,
\upomega:=\omega(\uplambda)\in K
, and suppose
\omega\big(\Uplambda(K)\big)<\upomega<\sigma\big(\Upgamma(K)\big)
. Then
\uplambda_{\rho}\leadsto\uplambda
, and the pre-
H
-subfield
K\langle\uplambda\rangle=K(\uplambda)
of
L
is an immediate extension of
K

(
and so
K\langle\uplambda\rangle
is not
\uplambda
-free
)
.

Proof.

From
\Uplambda(L)<\Updelta(L)
[ADH, p. 522] and
\Updelta(K)\subseteq\Updelta(L)
we
obtain
\uplambda<\Updelta(K)
.
The restriction of
\omega
to
\Uplambda(L)^{\downarrow}
is strictly increasing [ADH, p. 526] and
\Uplambda(K)\subseteq\Uplambda(L)
, so
\omega\big(\Uplambda(K)\big)<\upomega=\omega(\uplambda)
gives
\Uplambda(K)<\uplambda
.
Hence
\uplambda_{\rho}\leadsto\uplambda
by [ADH, 11.8.16]. Also
\upomega_{\rho}\leadsto\upomega
by [ADH, 11.8.30]. Thus
K\langle\uplambda\rangle
is an immediate extension of
K
by [ADH, 11.7.13].
∎

Achieving
\upomega
-freeness for pre-
H
-fields

In the rest of this section
H
is a pre-
H
-field and
L

is a Liouville closed
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic
H
-field extension of
H
.

Thus if
H
is
\upomega
-free, then so is
L
, by Theorem
I.3.1
.
The lemmas below give conditions guaranteeing that
L
is
\upomega
-free, while
H
is not.

Lemma I.3.18
.

Suppose
H
is grounded or has a gap. Then
L
is
\upomega
-free.

Proof.

Suppose
H
is grounded. Let
H_{\upomega}
be the
\upomega
-free pre-
H
-field extension of
H
introduced in connection with [ADH, 11.7.17] (where we use the letter
F
instead of
H
). Identifying
H_{\upomega}
with its image in
L
under an embedding
H_{\upomega}\to L
over
H
of pre-
H
-fields, we apply Theorem
I.3.1
to
K:=H_{\upomega}
to conclude that
L
is
\upomega
-free.

Next, suppose
H
has a gap
\beta=vb
,
b\in H^{\times}
. Take
a\in L

with
a^{\prime}=b
and
a\not\asymp 1
. Then
\alpha:=va
satisfies
\alpha^{\prime}=\beta
, and so the pre-
H
-field

H(a)\subseteq L
is grounded, by [ADH, 9.8.2 and remarks following its proof].
Now apply the previous case to
H(a)
in place of
H
.
∎

Lemma I.3.19
.

Suppose
H
has asymptotic integration and divisible value group, and
s\in H
creates a gap over
H
. Then
L
is
\upomega
-free.

Proof.

Take
f\in L^{\times}
with
f^{\dagger}=s
.
Then by [ADH, remark after 11.5.14],
vf
is a gap in
H\langle f\rangle=H(f)
, so
L
is
\upomega
-free by Lemma
I.3.18
applied to
H\langle f\rangle
in place of
H
.
∎

Lemma I.3.20
.

Suppose
H
is not
\uplambda
-free. Then
L
is
\upomega
-free.

Proof.

By [ADH, 11.6.8], the real closure
H^{\operatorname{rc}}
of
H
inside
L

is not
\uplambda
-free, hence replacing
H
by
H^{\operatorname{rc}}
we arrange that
H
is real closed.
If
H
does not have asymptotic integration, then we are done
by Lemma
I.3.18
. So suppose
H
has asymptotic integration. Then some

s\in H
creates a gap over
H
, by [ADH, 11.6.1], so
L
is
\upomega
-free by Lemma
I.3.19
.
∎

Corollary I.3.21
.

Suppose
H
is
\uplambda
-free and
\uplambda\in\Uplambda(L)^{\downarrow}
is such that
\upomega:=\omega(\uplambda)\in H
and

\omega\big(\Uplambda(H)\big)<\upomega<\sigma\big(\Upgamma(H)\big)
. Then
L
is
\upomega
-free.

Proof.

By Lemma
I.3.17
, the pre-
H
-subfield
H\langle\uplambda\rangle=H(\uplambda)
of
L
is
an immediate non-
\uplambda
-free extension of
H
. Now apply Lemma
I.3.20
to
H\langle\uplambda\rangle
in place of
H
.
∎

I.4.
Complements on Linear Differential Operators

In this section we tie up loose ends from the material on linear differential operators in [ADH, 14.2] and
[
3
, Section 8]
.

Throughout
K
is an ungrounded asymptotic field with
\Gamma=v(K^{\times})\neq\{0\}
,
a
,
b
,
f
,
g
,
h
range over arbitrary elements of
K
, and
\phi
over those active in
K
, in particular,
\phi\neq 0
.
Recall our use of the term “eventually”: a property
S(\phi)
of

\phi
is said to hold
eventually
if for some active
\phi_{0}
in
K
,
S(\phi)
holds for all
\phi\preccurlyeq\phi_{0}
.

We shall consider linear differential operators
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
and set
r:=\operatorname{order}(A)
.
In [ADH, 11.1] we showed that for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
the quantity
\operatorname{dwt}_{A^{\phi}}(\gamma)
is eventually constant; its eventual value is denoted by
\operatorname{nwt}_{A}(\gamma)
. We also introduced the set

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\ =\ {\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}_{K}(A)\ :=\ \big\{\gamma\in\Gamma:\,\operatorname{nwt}_{A}(\gamma)\geqslant 1\big\}\ =\ \bigcap_{\phi}{\mathscr{E}}(A^{\phi})

of
eventual exceptional values of
A
. For
a\neq 0
we have
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(aA)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(Aa)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)-va
. An easy consequence of the definitions:
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A^{f})={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
for
f\neq 0
.
A key fact about
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
is that if
y\in K^{\times}
,

vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, then
A(y)\asymp A^{\phi}y
, eventually.
Since
A^{\phi}y\neq 0
for
y\in K^{\times}
, this gives

v(\ker^{\neq}A)\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.

Lemma I.4.1
.

If
L
is an ungrounded asymptotic extension of
K
, then we have
{{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}_{L}(A)\cap\Gamma}\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, with equality if
\Psi
is cofinal in
\Psi_{L}
.

Proof.

For the inclusion, use that
\operatorname{dwt}(A^{\phi})
decreases as
v\phi
strictly increases [ADH, 11.1.12]. Thus its eventual value
\operatorname{nwt}(A)
, evaluated in
K
, cannot strictly increase when evaluated in an ungrounded asymptotic extension of
K
.
∎

In the rest of this section we assume in addition that
K
is
H
-asymptotic with asymptotic integration.

Then by [ADH, 14.2.8]:

Proposition I.4.2
.

If
K
is
r
-linearly newtonian, then
v(\ker^{\neq}A)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.

Remark I.4.3
.

If
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued, then
\lvert v(\ker^{\neq}A)\rvert=\dim_{C}\ker A\leqslant r
by [ADH, 5.6.6], using a reduction to the case of “small derivation” by compositional conjugation.

Corollary I.4.4
.

Suppose
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued,
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=v(\ker^{\neq}A)
, and
0\neq f\in A(K)
. Then
A(y)=f
for some
y\in K
with
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.

Proof.

Let
y\in K
,
A(y)=f
, with
vy
maximal. Then
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
:
otherwise we have
z\in\ker A
with
z\sim y
, so
A(y-z)=f
and
v(y-z)>vy
.
∎

Corollary I.4.5
.

Suppose
K
is
\upomega
-free. Then

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\operatorname{nwt}_{A}(\gamma)=\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\rvert\leqslant r
.

Proof.

By (
0.9
) we have
an immediate newtonian asymptotic extension
L
of
K
. Then
L
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued
by Lemma
I.2.9
, hence
\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\rvert=\lvert{\mathscr{E}}_{L}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\rvert\leqslant r
by Proposition
I.4.2
and Remark
I.4.3
.
By [ADH, 13.7.10] we have
\operatorname{nwt}_{A}(\gamma)\leqslant 1
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, thus
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\operatorname{nwt}_{A}(\gamma)=\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\rvert
.
∎

In [ADH, 11.1] we defined
{v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}\colon\Gamma\to\Gamma}
by requiring that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
:

(I.4.1)

v_{A^{\phi}}(\gamma)\ =\ v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)+\operatorname{nwt}_{A}(\gamma)v\phi,\qquad\text{ eventually.}

We recall from that reference that for
a\neq 0
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have

v_{aA}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\ =\ va+v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma),\qquad v_{Aa}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)=v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(va+\gamma).

As an example from [ADH, p. 481],
v_{\der}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)=\gamma+\psi(\gamma)
for
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
and
v_{\der}^{\operatorname{e}}(0)=0
.
By [ADH, 14.2.7 and the remark preceding it] we have:

Lemma I.4.6
.

The restriction of
v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}
to a function
\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\to\Gamma
is strictly increasing, and

v\big(A(y)\big)=v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(vy)
for all
y\in K
with
vy\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.
Moreover, if
K
is
\upomega
-free, then
v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}\big(\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\big)=\Gamma
.

A differential field
F
is said to be
r
-linearly surjective
(
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
) if
A(F)=F
for every
A\in F[\der]^{\neq}
of order at most
r
.
The following is [ADH, 14.2.10] without the hypothesis of
\upomega
-freeness:

Corollary I.4.7
.

Suppose
K
is
r
-linearly newtonian. Then for each
f\neq 0
there exists
y\in K^{\times}
such that
A(y)=f
,

vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, and
v^{\operatorname{e}}_{A}(vy)=vf
.

Proof.

If
r=0
, then
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=\emptyset
and our claim is obviously valid. Suppose
r\geqslant 1
. Then

K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued by Lemma
I.2.9
, and
v(\ker^{\neq}A)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)

by Proposition
I.4.2
, Moreover, by [ADH, 14.2.2],
K
is
r
-linearly surjective, hence
f\in A(K)
.
Now Corollary
I.4.4
yields
y\in K^{\times}
with
A(y)=f
and
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.
By Lemma
I.4.6
we have
v^{\operatorname{e}}_{A}(vy)=v\big(A(y)\big)=vf
.
∎

From the proof of [ADH, 14.2.10] we extract the following:

Corollary I.4.8
.

Suppose
K
is
r
-linearly newtonian with small derivation, and
A\in~\mathcal{O}[\der]
with
a_{0}:=A(1)\asymp 1
,
and
f\asymp^{\flat}1
. Then there is
y\in K^{\times}
such that
{A(y)=f}
and
y\sim f/a_{0}
. For any such
y
we have
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)

and
v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(vy)=vf
.

Proof.

The case
r=0
is trivial.
Assume
r\geqslant 1
, so
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued by Lemma
I.2.9
. Hence
f^{\dagger}\prec 1
, that is,
f^{\prime}\prec f
,
so
f^{(n)}\prec f
for all
n\geqslant 1
by [ADH, 4.4.2]. Then
Af\preccurlyeq~f
by [ADH, (5.1.3), (5.1.2)], and
A(f)\sim a_{0}f
, so

A_{\ltimes f}\in\mathcal{O}[\der]
and
A_{\ltimes f}(1)\sim~a_{0}
. Thus we may replace
A
,
f
by
A_{\ltimes f}
,
1
to arrange
{f=1}
.
Now
{a_{0}\asymp 1}
gives
\operatorname{dwm}(A)=0
, so
\operatorname{dwt}(A^{\phi})=0
eventually, by [ADH, 11.1.11(ii)],
that is,
\operatorname{nwt}(A)=0
. Also
A^{\phi}(1)=A(1)=a_{0}\asymp 1
, so
v^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=0
. Arguing as in the proof of [ADH, 14.2.10] we obtain

y\in K^{\times}
with
A(y)=1
and
y\sim 1/a_{0}
. It is clear that
vy=0\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
and
v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(vy)=v^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=0=vf

for any such
y
.
∎

In the next few subsections below we consider more closely the case of order
r=1
, and in the last subsection the case of arbitrary order.

First-order operators

In this subsection
A=\der-g
. By [ADH, p. 481],

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\ =\ {\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}_{K}(A)\ =\ \big\{vy:\,y\in K^{\times},\ v(g-y^{\dagger})>\Psi\big\}

has at most one element.
We also have

\lvert v(\ker^{\neq}A)\rvert=\dim_{C}\ker A\leqslant 1
in view of
C^{\times}\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{\times}
. Proposition
I.4.2
holds under a weaker assumption on
K
for
r=1
:

Lemma I.4.9
.

Suppose
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
. Then
v(\ker^{\neq}A)={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.

Proof.

It remains to show “
\supseteq
”.
Suppose
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=\{0\}
. Then
g-y^{\dagger}\in\operatorname{I}(K)
with
{y\asymp 1}
in
K
, hence

g\in\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, so
g=h^{\dagger}
with
h\asymp 1
, and thus
0=vh\in v(\ker^{\neq}A)
. The general case reduces to the case
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=\{0\}
by twisting.
∎

Lemma I.4.10
.

Suppose
L
is an ungrounded
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
. Then

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}_{L}(A)\cap\Gamma={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.

Proof.

Lemma
I.4.1
gives

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}_{L}(A)\cap\Gamma\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.
Next, let
vy\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
,
y\in K^{\times}
.
Then
v(g-y^{\dagger})>\Psi
and so
v(g-y^{\dagger})\in(\Gamma^{>})^{\prime}
since
K
has asymptotic integration.
Hence
v(g-y^{\dagger})>\Psi_{L}
and thus
vy\in{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}_{L}(A)
, by [ADH, p. 481].
∎

Recall also from [ADH, 9.7] that for an ordered abelian group
G
and
U\subseteq G
, a function
\eta\colon U\to G
is said to be
slowly varying
if
\eta(\alpha)-\eta(\beta)=o(\alpha-\beta)
for all
\alpha\neq\beta
in
U
; then the function

\gamma\mapsto\gamma+\eta(\gamma)\colon U\to G
is strictly increasing. The quintessential example of a slowly varying function
is
\psi\colon\Gamma^{\neq}\to\Gamma
[ADH, 6.5.4(ii)].

Proposition I.4.11
.

There is a unique slowly varying function
\psi_{A}\colon\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\to\Gamma

such that for all
y\in K^{\times}
with
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
we have
v\big(A(y)\big)=vy+\psi_{A}(vy)
.

Proof.

For
\operatorname{d}
-valued
K
, use
[
3
, 8.4]
. In general, pass to the
\operatorname{d}
-valued hull
L:=\operatorname{dv}(K)
of
K
from
[ADH, 10.3] and use
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma
[ADH, 10.3.2].
∎

If
b\neq 0
, then
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A_{\ltimes b})={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)-vb
and
\psi_{A_{\ltimes b}}(\gamma)=\psi_{A}(\gamma+vb)
for
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A_{\ltimes b})
.

Example
.

We have

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(\der)=\{0\}
and

\psi_{\der}=\psi
.
More generally, if
g=b^{\dagger}
,
b\neq 0
, then
A_{\ltimes b}=\der
and so
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=\{vb\}
and

\psi_{A}(\gamma)=\psi(\gamma-vb)
for
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{vb\}
.

If
\Gamma
is divisible, then
\Gamma\setminus v\big(A(K)\big)
has at most one element by [ADH, 11.6.16]. Also,

K
is
\uplambda
-free iff
v\big(A(K)\big)=\Gamma_{\infty}
for all
A=\der-g
by [ADH, 11.6.17].

Lemma I.4.12
.

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
f\neq 0
. Then for some
y\in K^{\times}
we have
{A(y)\asymp f}
and
{vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)}
.

(
Hence
\gamma\mapsto\gamma+\psi_{A}(\gamma)\colon\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\to\Gamma
is surjective.
)

Proof.

[ADH, 11.6.17] gives
y\in K^{\times}
with
A^{\phi}y\asymp f
eventually. Now

A^{\phi}y\ =\ \phi y\derdelta-(g-y^{\dagger})y\ \text{ in }K^{\phi}[\derdelta],\qquad\derdelta:=\phi^{-1}\der.

Since
v(A^{\phi}y)=vf
eventually, this forces
g-y^{\dagger}\succ\phi
eventually, so
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.
∎

Call
A

steep
if
g\succ^{\flat}1
, that is,
g\succ 1
and
g^{\dagger}\succcurlyeq 1
. If
K
has small derivation and

A
is steep, then
g^{\dagger}\prec g
by [ADH, 9.2.10].

Lemma I.4.13
.

Suppose
K
has small derivation,
A
is steep, and
y\in K^{\times}
such that
A(y)=f\neq 0
,
g\succ f^{\dagger}
, and
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
. Then
y\sim-f/g
.

Proof.

We have

(f/g)^{\dagger}-g=f^{\dagger}-g^{\dagger}-g\sim-g\succ g^{\dagger},

hence
v(f/g)\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, and

A(f/g)\ =\ (f/g)^{\prime}-(f/g)g\ =\ (f/g)\cdot\big(f^{\dagger}-g^{\dagger}-g\big)\ \sim\ (f/g)\cdot(-g)\ =\ -f.

Since
A(y)=f\sim A(-f/g)
and
vy,v(f/g)\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, this gives
y=u\cdot f/g
where
u\asymp 1
, by Proposition
I.4.11
.
Now
u^{\dagger}\prec 1\prec g
and
(f/g)^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}-g^{\dagger}\prec g
,
hence
y^{\dagger}\prec g
and so

f=A(y)=y\cdot(y^{\dagger}-g)\sim-yg.

Therefore
y\sim-f/g
.
∎

Lemma I.4.14
.

Suppose
K
has small derivation and
y\in K^{\times}
is such that
A(y)=f\neq 0
,
g-f^{\dagger}\succ^{\flat}1
and
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
. Then

y\sim f/(f^{\dagger}-g)
.

Proof.

From
g-f^{\dagger}\succ 1
we get
vf\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
. Now
A(y)=f\prec f(f^{\dagger}-g)=A(f)
, so
y\prec f
by [ADH, 5.6.8], and

v(y/f)\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A_{\ltimes f})={\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)-vf
. Since
A_{\ltimes f}=\der-(g-f^{\dagger})
is steep,
Lemma
I.4.13
applies to
A_{\ltimes f}
,
y/f
,
1
in the role of
A
,
y
,
f
.
∎

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
f\neq 0
. Then [ADH, 11.6.1] gives an active
\phi_{0}
in
K
with
{f^{\dagger}-g-\phi^{\dagger}}\succcurlyeq\phi_{0}

for all
\phi\prec\phi_{0}
. The convex subgroups
\Gamma^{\flat}_{\phi}
of
\Gamma
become arbitrarily small as we let
v\phi
increase cofinally
in
\Psi^{\downarrow}
, so
\phi\prec^{\flat}_{\phi}\phi_{0}
eventually, and hence
f^{\dagger}-g-\phi^{\dagger}\succ^{\flat}_{\phi}\phi
eventually, that is,

\phi^{-1}(f/\phi)^{\dagger}-g/\phi\succ^{\flat}_{\phi}1
eventually. So replacing
K
by
K^{\phi}
,
A
by

\phi^{-1}A^{\phi}=\derdelta-(g/\phi)
in
K^{\phi}[\derdelta]
,
and
f
and
g
by
f/\phi
and
g/\phi
, for suitable
\phi
, we arrange
f^{\dagger}-g\succ^{\flat}1
.
Thus by Lemma
I.4.14
:

Corollary I.4.15
.

If
K
is
\uplambda
-free,
y\in K^{\times}
,
A(y)=f\neq 0
, and
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, then
y\sim f/\big((f/\phi)^{\dagger}-g\big)
, eventually.

Example
.

If
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
y\in K
,
y^{\prime}=f\neq 0
with
y\not\asymp 1
, then
y\sim f/(f/\phi)^{\dagger}
, eventually.

From
K
to
K[\imag]

In this subsection
K
is a real closed
H
-field
.
Then
K[\imag]
(
{\imag^{2}=-1}
) is an
H
-asymptotic extension of
K
, with
\Gamma_{K[\imag]}=\Gamma
.
Consider a linear differential operator
B=\der-(g+h\imag)
over
K[\imag]
.
Note that
g+h\imag\in K[\imag]^{\dagger}
iff
g\in K^{\dagger}
and
{h\imag\in K[\imag]^{\dagger}}
, by
Lemma
I.2.4
.
Under further assumptions on
K
, the next two results give explicit descriptions of
\psi_{B}
when
g\in K^{\dagger}
.

Proposition I.4.16
.

Suppose
K[\imag]
is
1
-linearly newtonian and
g\in K^{\dagger}
. Then:

\rm(i)

if
h\imag\in K[\imag]^{\dagger}
, then for some
\beta\in\Gamma
we have

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)\ =\ \{\beta\},\qquad\psi_{B}(\gamma)\ =\ \psi(\gamma-\beta)\ \text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{\beta\};

\rm(ii)

if
h\imag\notin K[\imag]^{\dagger}
and
g=b^{\dagger}
,

b\neq 0
, then

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)\ =\ \emptyset,\qquad\psi_{B}(\gamma)\ =\ \min\!\big(\psi(\gamma-vb),vh\big)\ \text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma.

Proof.

As to (i), apply the example following Proposition
I.4.11
to
K[\imag]
,
B
,
g+h\imag
in the roles
of
K
,
A
,
g
. For (ii), assume
h\imag\notin K[\imag]^{\dagger}
,
g=b^{\dagger}
,
b\neq 0
.
Replacing
B
by
B_{\ltimes b}
we arrange
g=0
,
b=1
,
B=\der-h\imag
.
Corollary
I.2.14
gives
K[\imag]^{\dagger}=K^{\dagger}\oplus\operatorname{I}(K)\imag
, so
h\notin\operatorname{I}(K)
, and thus
vh\in\Psi^{\downarrow}
.
Let
y\in K[\imag]^{\times}
, and take
z\in K^{\times}
and
s\in\operatorname{I}(K)
with
y^{\dagger}=z^{\dagger}+s\imag
. Then

vh<vs
, hence

v(y^{\dagger}-h\imag)\ =\ \min\!\big(v(z^{\dagger}),v(s-h)\big)\ =\ \min\!\big(v(z^{\dagger}),vs,vh\big)\ =\ \min\!\big(v(y^{\dagger}),vh\big),

where the last equality uses
v(y^{\dagger})=\min\big(v(z^{\dagger}),vs\big)
. Thus
v(y^{\dagger}-h\imag)\in\Psi^{\downarrow}
and

v\big(B(y)\big)-vy\ =\ v(y^{\dagger}-h\imag)\ =\ \min\!\big(v(y^{\dagger}),vh\big)\ =\ \min\!\big(\psi(vy),vh\big),

which gives the desired result.
∎

Corollary I.4.17
.

Suppose
K
is
\upomega
-free,
g\in K^{\dagger}
,
g=b^{\dagger}
,
b\neq 0
. Then either for some
\beta\in\Gamma
we have
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)=\{\beta\}
and
\psi_{B}(\gamma)=\psi(\gamma-\beta)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{\beta\}
, or
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)=\emptyset
and

\psi_{B}(\gamma)=\min\!\big(\psi(\gamma-vb),vh\big)
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

By (
0.9
) we have an immediate newtonian extension
L
of
K
. Then
L
is still a real closed
H
-field [ADH, 10.5.8, 3.5.19], and
L[\imag]
is newtonian by (
0.12
), so Proposition
I.4.16
applies to
L
in place of
K
.
∎

Higher-order operators

We begin with the following observation:

Lemma I.4.18
.

Let
B\in K[\der]^{\neq}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Then

\operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)\geqslant\operatorname{nwt}_{B}(\gamma)
, and

\gamma\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)\ \Longrightarrow\ \operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)\ =\ \operatorname{nwt}_{A}\!\big(v^{\operatorname{e}}_{B}(\gamma)\big)\text{ and }\ v^{\operatorname{e}}_{AB}(\gamma)\ =\ v^{\operatorname{e}}_{A}\big(v^{\operatorname{e}}_{B}(\gamma)\big).

Proof.

We have

\operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)=\operatorname{dwt}_{(AB)^{\phi}}(\gamma)
eventually, and

(AB)^{\phi}=A^{\phi}B^{\phi}
. Hence by [ADH, 5.6] and the definition of
v^{\operatorname{e}}_{B}(\gamma)
in (
I.4.1
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)\

\displaystyle=\ \operatorname{dwt}_{A^{\phi}}\!\big(v_{B^{\phi}}(\gamma)\big)+\operatorname{dwt}_{B^{\phi}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\ \operatorname{dwt}_{A^{\phi}}\!\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)+\operatorname{nwt}_{B}(\gamma)v\phi\big)+\operatorname{nwt}_{B}(\gamma),\text{ eventually},

so
\operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)\geqslant\operatorname{nwt}_{B}(\gamma)
. Now suppose
\gamma\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)
. Then
\operatorname{nwt}_{B}(\gamma)=0
, so

\operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)=\operatorname{dwt}_{A^{\phi}}\!\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)=\operatorname{nwt}_{A}\!\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big),\qquad\text{eventually.}

Moreover,
v_{(AB)^{\phi}}=v_{A^{\phi}B^{\phi}}=v_{A^{\phi}}\circ v_{B^{\phi}}
, hence using (
I.4.1
):

v_{(AB)^{\phi}}(\gamma)\ =\ v_{A^{\phi}}\big(v_{B^{\phi}}(\gamma)\big)\ =\ v_{A^{\phi}}\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big),\text{ eventually},

and thus eventually

\displaystyle v^{\operatorname{e}}_{AB}(\gamma)\

\displaystyle=\ v_{(AB)^{\phi}}(\gamma)-\operatorname{nwt}_{AB}(\gamma)v\phi

\displaystyle=\ v_{A^{\phi}}\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)-\operatorname{nwt}_{A}\!\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)v\phi\ =\ v^{\operatorname{e}}_{A}\big(v^{\operatorname{e}}_{B}(\gamma)\big).\qed

Lemmas
I.4.6
and
I.4.18
yield:

Corollary I.4.19
.

Let
B\in K[\der]^{\neq}
. Then

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(AB)\ =\ (v^{\operatorname{e}}_{B})^{-1}\big({\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\big)\cup{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)

and hence

\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(AB)\rvert\leqslant\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\rvert+\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)\rvert
,
with equality if
v^{\operatorname{e}}_{B}\big(\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)\big)=\Gamma
.

As an easy consequence we have a variant of Corollary
I.4.5
:

Corollary I.4.20
.

If
A
splits over
K
, then
\lvert{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\rvert\leqslant r
.

To study
v^{\operatorname{e}}_{A}
in more detail we introduce the function

\psi_{A}\ \colon\ \Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\to\Gamma,\qquad\gamma\mapsto v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)-\gamma.

For monic
A
of order
1
this agrees with
\psi_{A}
as defined in Proposition
I.4.11
.
For
{A=a}
(
a\neq 0
) we have
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)=\emptyset
and
\psi_{A}(\gamma)=va
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Lemma I.4.21
.

Let
B\in K[\der]^{\neq}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(AB)
. Then

\psi_{AB}(\gamma)\ =\ \psi_{A}\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)+\psi_{B}(\gamma).

Proof.

We have
\gamma\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)
and
v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
by Corollary
I.4.19
, hence

\psi_{AB}(\gamma)\ =\ v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)-\gamma\ =\ v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)+\psi_{A}\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)-\gamma\ =\ \psi_{A}\big(v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\gamma)\big)+\psi_{B}(\gamma)

by Lemma
I.4.18
.
∎

Thus for
a\neq 0
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have

\psi_{aA}(\gamma)=va+\psi_{A}(\gamma)\text{ if $\gamma\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)$,}\quad\psi_{Aa}(\gamma)=\psi_{A}(va+\gamma)+va\text{ if
$\gamma\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)-va$.}

Example
.

Suppose
K
has small derivation and
x\in K
,
x^{\prime}\asymp 1
. Then
vx<0
and
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(\der^{2})=\{vx,0\}
, and
\psi_{\der^{2}}(\gamma)=\psi\big(\gamma+\psi(\gamma)\big)+\psi(\gamma)
for
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(\der^{2})
.

Lemma I.4.22
.

Suppose
\psi_{A}
is slowly varying. Let
\Delta
be a convex subgroup of
\Gamma
and let
y,z\in K^{\times}
be such that
vy,vz\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
.
Then

v_{\Delta}(y)<v_{\Delta}(z)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ v_{\Delta}\big(A(y)\big)<v_{\Delta}\big(A(z)\big).

Proof.

By Lemma
I.4.6
we have

v\big(A(y)\big)-v\big(A(z)\big)\ =\ v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(vy)-v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(vz)\ =\ vy-vz+\psi_{A}(vy)-\psi_{A}(vz)

and
\psi_{A}(vy)-\psi_{A}(vz)=o(vy-vz)
if
vy\neq vz
.
∎

Call
A

asymptotically surjective

if
v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}\big(\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)\big)=\Gamma
and

\psi_{A}
is slowly varying.
If
A
is asymptotically surjective, then so are
aA
and
Aa
for
a\neq 0
, and
if
A
has order
0
, then
A
is asymptotically surjective.
If
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
A
has order
1
, then
A
is asymptotically surjective, thanks to Proposition
I.4.11
and Lemma
I.4.12
.

The next lemma has an obvious proof.

Lemma I.4.23
.

Let
G
be an ordered abelian group and
U,V\subseteq G
. If
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\colon U\to G
are slowly varying,
then so is
\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}
. If
\eta\colon U\to G
and
\zeta\colon V\to G

are slowly varying and
\gamma+\zeta(\gamma)\in U
for all
\gamma\in V
, then the function
\gamma\mapsto\eta\big(\gamma+\zeta(\gamma)\big)\colon V\to G
is also slowly varying.

Lemma I.4.24
.

If
A
and
B\in K[\der]^{\neq}
are asymptotically surjective, then so is
AB
.

Proof.

Let
A
,
B
be asymptotically surjective and
\gamma\in\Gamma
. This gives
\alpha\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)

with
v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(\alpha)=\gamma
and
\beta\in\Gamma\setminus{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(B)

with
v_{B}^{\operatorname{e}}(\beta)=\alpha
. Then
\beta\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(AB)

by Corollary
I.4.19
, and
v_{AB}^{\operatorname{e}}(\beta)=\gamma
by Lemma
I.4.18
. Moreover,
\psi_{AB}
is slowly varying
by Lemmas
I.4.21
and
I.4.23
.
∎

A straightforward induction on
r
using this lemma yields:

Corollary I.4.25
.

If
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
A
splits over
K
, then
A
is asymptotically surjective.

We can now add to Lemma
I.4.6
:

Corollary I.4.26
.

Suppose
K
is
\upomega
-free. Then
A
is asymptotically surjective.

Proof.

By the second part of Lemma
I.4.6
it is enough to show that
\psi_{A}
is slowly varying.
For this we may replace
K
by any
\upomega
-free extension
L
of
K
with
\Psi
cofinal in
\Psi_{L}
.
Thus we can arrange by (
0.9
) and (
0.12
) that
K
is newtonian, and by passing
to the algebraic closure, algebraically closed. Then
A
splits over
K
by (
0.10
) and Lemma
I.2.9
,
so
A
is asymptotically surjective by Corollary
I.4.25
.
∎

I.5.
Special Elements

Let
K
be a valued field and let
\widehat{a}
be an element of an immediate extension of
K
with
\widehat{a}\notin K
.
Recall that

v(\widehat{a}-K)\ =\ \big\{v(\widehat{a}-a):a\in K\big\}

is a nonempty downward closed subset of
\Gamma:=v(K^{\times})
without a largest element.
Call
\widehat{a}

special
over
K
if
some nontrivial convex subgroup of
\Gamma
is cofinal in
v({\widehat{a}-K})
[ADH, p. 167].
In this case
v(\widehat{a}-K)\cap\Gamma^{>}\neq\emptyset
, and there is a unique such nontrivial convex subgroup
\Delta
of
\Gamma
,
namely

\Delta\ =\ \big\{\delta\in\Gamma:\,\lvert\delta\rvert\in v(\widehat{a}-K)\big\}.

We also call
\widehat{a}

almost special
over
K
if
\widehat{a}/{\mathfrak{m}}
is special over
K
for some
{\mathfrak{m}}\in K^{\times}
.
If
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
is archimedean, then
\widehat{a}
is special over
K
iff
v(\widehat{a}-K)=\Gamma
, iff
\widehat{a}
is the limit of a divergent c-sequence in
K
. (Recall that “c-sequence” abbreviates “cauchy sequence” [ADH, p. 82].)
In the next lemma
a
ranges over
K
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
,
{\mathfrak{n}}
over
K^{\times}
.

Lemma I.5.1
.

Suppose
\widehat{a}\prec{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\widehat{a}/{\mathfrak{m}}
is special over
K
. Then for all
a
,
{\mathfrak{n}}
, if
\widehat{a}-a\prec{\mathfrak{n}}\preccurlyeq{\mathfrak{m}}
, then
(\widehat{a}-a)/{\mathfrak{n}}
is special over
K
.

Proof.

Replacing
\widehat{a}
,
a
,
{\mathfrak{m}}
,
{\mathfrak{n}}
by
\widehat{a}/{\mathfrak{m}}
,
a/{\mathfrak{m}}
,
1
,
{\mathfrak{n}}/{\mathfrak{m}}
, respectively, we arrange
{\mathfrak{m}}=1
. So let
\widehat{a}
be special over
K
with
\widehat{a}\prec 1
. It is enough to show: (1)
\widehat{a}-a
is special over
K
, for all
a
; (2) for all
{\mathfrak{n}}
, if
\widehat{a}\prec{\mathfrak{n}}\preccurlyeq 1
, then
\widehat{a}/{\mathfrak{n}}
is special over
K
. Here (1) follows from
v(\widehat{a}-a-K)=v(\widehat{a}-K)
. For (2), note that if
\widehat{a}\prec{\mathfrak{n}}\preccurlyeq 1
,
then
v{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Delta
with
\Delta
as above, and so
v(\widehat{a}/{\mathfrak{n}}-K)=v(\widehat{a}-K)-v{\mathfrak{n}}=v(\widehat{a}-K)
.
∎

The remainder of this section is devoted to showing that (almost) special elements arise naturally in the analysis of
certain immediate
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic extensions of valued differential fields.
We first treat the case of asymptotic fields with small derivation, and then
focus on the linearly newtonian
H
-asymptotic case.

We recall some notation: for an ordered abelian group
\Gamma
and
\alpha\in\Gamma_{\infty}
,

{\beta\in\Gamma}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma^{>}

we mean by “
\alpha\geqslant\beta+o(\gamma)
” that

\alpha\geqslant\beta-(1/n)\gamma
for all
n\geqslant 1
, while “
{\alpha<\beta+o(\gamma)}
” is its negation, that is,

\alpha<\beta-(1/n)\gamma
for some
n\geqslant 1
; see [ADH, p. 312]. Here and later inequalities are
in the sense of the ordered divisible hull
{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
of the relevant
\Gamma
.

A source of special elements

In this subsection
K
is an asymptotic field with small derivation, value group
\Gamma=v(K^{\times})\neq\{0\}
, and differential residue field
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
; we also let
r\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
.
Below we use the notion
neatly surjective
from [ADH, 5.6]:
{A\in K[\der]^{\neq}}
is neatly surjective iff for all
b\in K^{\times}
there exists
a\in K^{\times}
with
A(a)=b
and
v_{A}(va)=vb
.
For use in the next proof, recall from [ADH, 7.1] the notion of a valued differential field being
r
-differential-henselian
(or
r
-
\operatorname{d}
-henselian,
for short).
We often let
\widehat{f}
be an element in an immediate asymptotic extension
\widehat{K}
of
K
, but in the statement of the next lemma we take
\widehat{f}\in K
:

Lemma I.5.2
.

Assume
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
r
-linearly surjective,
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
of order
\leqslant r
is neatly surjective,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
,
\gamma>0
,
\widehat{f}\in K^{\times}
, and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant v(A\widehat{f})+\gamma
. Then
A(f)=0
and
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
for some
f\in K
.

Proof.

Set
B:=g^{-1}A\widehat{f}
, where we take
g\in K^{\times}
such that
vg=v(A\widehat{f})
. Then
B\asymp 1
,
B
is still neatly surjective, and
B(1)=g^{-1}A(\widehat{f})
,
v\big(B(1)\big)\geqslant\gamma
. It suffices to find
y\in K
such that
B(y)=0
and
v(y-1)\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma)
, because then
f:=\widehat{f}y
has the desired property.
If
B(1)=0
, then
y=1
works, so assume
B(1)\neq 0
. By [ADH, 7.2.7] we have an immediate extension
\widehat{K}
of
K
that is
r
-differential henselian. Then
\widehat{K}
is asymptotic by [ADH, 9.4.2 and 9.4.5].
Set
R(Z):=\operatorname{Ri}(B)\in K\{Z\}
.
Then the proof of [ADH, 7.5.1] applied to
\widehat{K}
and
B
in the roles
of
K
and
A
yields
z\prec 1
in
\widehat{K}
with
R(z)=0
. Now
R(0)=B(1)
,
hence by [ADH, 7.2.2] we can take such
z
with
v(z)\geqslant\beta+o(\beta)
where
\beta:=v\big(B(1)\big)\geqslant\gamma
.
As in the proof of
[ADH, 7.5.1] we next take
y\in\widehat{K}
with
v(y-1)>0
and

y^{\dagger}=z
to get
B(y)=0
, and observe that then

v(y-1)\geqslant\beta+o(\beta)
, by [ADH, 9.2.10(iv)], hence
v(y-1)\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma)
. It remains to note that
y\in K
by [ADH, 7.5.7].
∎

By a remark following the proof of [ADH, 7.5.1] the assumption that
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
r
-linearly surjective in the lemma above can be replaced for
r\geqslant 2
by the assumption that
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
(r-1)
-linearly surjective.

Next we establish a version of the above with
\widehat{f}
in an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
. Recall that an asymptotic extension of
K
with the same value group as
K
has small derivation, by [ADH, 9.4.1].

Lemma I.5.3
.

Assume
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
r
-linearly surjective,
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
of order
\leqslant r
is neatly surjective,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
,
\gamma>0
,
\widehat{K}
is an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
,
\widehat{f}\in\widehat{K}^{\times}
, and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant v(A\widehat{f})+\gamma
. Then for some
f\in K
we have

A(f)\ =\ 0,\qquad v(\widehat{f}-f)\ \geqslant\ v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma).

Proof.

By extending
\widehat{K}
we can arrange that

\widehat{K}
is
r
-differential henselian,
so
A
remains neatly surjective as an element of
\widehat{K}[\der]
, by [ADH, 7.1.8]. Then by Lemma
I.5.2
with
\widehat{K}
in the role of
K
we get
f\in\widehat{K}
such that
A(f)=0
and

v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
. It remains to note that

f\in K
by [ADH, 7.5.7].
∎

We actually need an inhomogeneous variant of the above:

Lemma I.5.4
.

Assume
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
r
-linearly surjective,
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
of order
\leqslant r
is neatly surjective,
b\in K
,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
,
\gamma>0
,
v(A)=o(\gamma)
,
v(b)\geqslant o(\gamma)
,
\widehat{K}
is an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
,
\widehat{f}\in\widehat{K}
,

\widehat{f}\preccurlyeq 1
, and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})-b\big)\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma)
.
Then

A(f)\ =\ b,\qquad v(\widehat{f}-f)\ \geqslant\ (1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)

for some
f\in K
.

Proof.

Take
y\in K
with
A(y)=b
and
v(y)\geqslant o(\gamma)
. Then
A(\widehat{g})=A(\widehat{f})-b
for
\widehat{g}:=\widehat{f}-y
, so
v\big(A(\widehat{g})\big)\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma)
and
v(\widehat{g})\geqslant o(\gamma)
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)
v(\widehat{g})\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Then
v(\widehat{f}-y)\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
, so
f:=y
works.

(2)
v(\widehat{g})<(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Then by [ADH, 6.1.3],

v(A\widehat{g})\ <\ (1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma),\qquad v\big(A(\widehat{g})\big)\ \geqslant\ \gamma+o(\gamma),

so

v\big(A(\widehat{g})\big)\geqslant v(A\widehat{g})+(1/2)\gamma
. Then
Lemma
I.5.3

gives an element
g\in K
such that
A(g)=0
and

v(\widehat{g}-g)\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Hence
f:=y+g
works.
∎

Recall from [ADH, 7.2] that
\mathcal{O}
is said to be
r
-linearly surjective
if for every
A
in
K[\der]^{\neq}
of order
r
with
v(A)=0
there exists
y\in\mathcal{O}
with
A(y)=1
.

Proposition I.5.5
.

Assume
\mathcal{O}
is
r
-linearly surjective,

P\in K\{Y\}
,
\operatorname{order}(P)\leqslant r
,
\operatorname{ddeg}P=1
,
and
P(\widehat{a})=0
, where
\widehat{a}\preccurlyeq 1
lies in an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
and
\widehat{a}\notin K
. Then
\widehat{a}
is special over
K
.

Proof.

The hypothesis on
\mathcal{O}
yields:
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
r
-linearly surjective and all
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}

of order
\leqslant r
are neatly surjective. Let
0<\gamma\in v(\widehat{a}-K)
; we claim that
v(\widehat{a}-K)
has an element
\geqslant(4/3)\gamma
.
We arrange
P\asymp 1
. Take
a\in K
with
v(\widehat{a}-a)=\gamma
. Then
{P_{+a}\asymp 1}
,
\operatorname{ddeg}P_{+a}=1
, so

P_{+a,1}\ \asymp\ 1,\quad P_{+a,>1}\ \prec\ 1,\quad P_{+a}\ =\ P(a)+P_{+a,1}+P_{+a,>1}

and

0\ =\ P(\widehat{a})\ =\ P_{+a}(\widehat{a}-a)\ =\ P(a)+P_{+a,1}(\widehat{a}-a)+P_{+a,>1}(\widehat{a}-a),

with

v\big(P_{+a,1}(\widehat{a}-a)+P_{+a,>1}(\widehat{a}-a)\big)\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma),

and thus
v(P(a))\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma)
.
Take
g\in K^{\times}
with
vg=\gamma
and set
Q:=g^{-1}P_{+a,\times g}
, so
Q=Q_{0}+Q_{1}+Q_{>1}
with

Q_{0}\ =\ Q(0)\ =\ g^{-1}P(a),\quad Q_{1}\ =\ g^{-1}(P_{+a,1})_{\times g},\quad Q_{>1}\ =\ g^{-1}(P_{+a,>1})_{\times g},

hence

v(Q_{0})\geqslant o(\gamma),\quad v(Q_{1})=o(\gamma),\quad v(Q_{>1})\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma).

We set
\widehat{f}:=g^{-1}(\widehat{a}-a)
, so
Q(\widehat{f})=0
and
\widehat{f}\asymp 1
, and
A:=L_{Q}\in K[\der]
. Then
Q(\widehat{f})=0
gives

Q_{0}+A(\widehat{f})\ =\ Q_{0}+Q_{1}(\widehat{f})\ =\ -Q_{>1}(\widehat{f}),\ \text{ with }v\big(Q_{>1}(\widehat{f})\big)\ \geqslant\ \gamma+o(\gamma),

so
v\big(Q_{0}+A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Since
v(A)=v(Q_{1})=o(\gamma)
, Lemma
I.5.4
then gives
f\in K
with
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant(1/3)\gamma
.
In view of
\widehat{a}-a=g\widehat{f}
, this yields

v\big(\widehat{a}-(a+gf)\big)\ =\ \gamma+v(\widehat{f}-f)\ \geqslant\ (4/3)\gamma,

which proves our claim. It gives the desired result.
∎

A source of almost special elements

In this subsection

K
,
\Gamma
,
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
, and
r
are as in the previous subsection, and we assume that
\mathcal{O}
is

r
-linearly surjective.
(So
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is
r
-linearly surjective, and
\sup\Psi=0
by [ADH, 9.4.2].)
Let

\widehat{a}
be an element in an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
such that
\widehat{a}\notin K
and

K\langle\widehat{a}\rangle
has transcendence degree
\leqslant r

over
K
. We shall use Proposition
I.5.5
to show:

Proposition I.5.6
.

If
\Gamma
is divisible, then
\widehat{a}
is almost special over
K
.

Towards the proof we first note that
\widehat{a}
has a minimal annihilator
P(Y)

over
K
of order
\leqslant r
. We also fix a divergent pc-sequence
(a_{\rho})
in
K
such that
a_{\rho}\leadsto\widehat{a}
.
(See [ADH, 4.1] for “minimal annihilator”, and [ADH, 4.4] for “minimal differential polynomial of
(a_{\rho})
over
K
”.)
We next show how to improve
\widehat{a}
and
P
(without assuming divisibility of
\Gamma
):

Lemma I.5.7
.

For some
\widehat{b}
in an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
we have:

(i)

v(\widehat{a}-K)=v(\widehat{b}-K)
;

(ii)

(a_{\rho})
has a minimal differential polynomial
Q
over
K
of order
\leqslant r
such that
Q

is also a minimal annihilator of
\widehat{b}
over
K
.

Proof.

By [ADH, 6.8.1, 6.9.2],
(a_{\rho})
is of
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic type over
K
with a minimal differential polynomial
Q
over
K
such that
\operatorname{order}Q\leqslant\operatorname{order}P\leqslant r
.
By [ADH, 6.9.3, 9.4.5] this gives an element
\widehat{b}
in an immediate asymptotic extension of
K
such that
Q
is a minimal annihilator of
\widehat{b}
over
K
and
a_{\rho}\leadsto\widehat{b}
. Then
Q
and
\widehat{b}
have the desired properties.
∎

Proof of Proposition
I.5.6
.

Replace
\widehat{a}
and
P

by
\widehat{b}
and
Q
from Lemma
I.5.7
(and rename) to arrange
that
P
is a minimal differential polynomial of
(a_{\rho})
over
K
.
Now assuming
\Gamma
is divisible,
[
22
, Proposition 3.1]
gives
a\in K
and
g\in K^{\times}
such that
\widehat{a}-a\asymp g
and
\operatorname{ddeg}P_{+a,\times g}=1
.

Set
F:=P_{+a,\times g}
and

\widehat{f}:=(\widehat{a}-a)/g
. Then
\operatorname{ddeg}F=1
,
F(\widehat{f})=0
, and
\widehat{f}\preccurlyeq 1
. Applying Proposition
I.5.5
to

F
and
\widehat{f}
in the role of
P
and
\widehat{a}
yields a
nontrivial convex subgroup
\Delta
of
\Gamma
that is
cofinal in
v(\widehat{f}-K)
. Setting
\alpha:=vg
, it follows that
\alpha+\Delta
is cofinal in
v\big((\widehat{a}-a)-K\big)=v(\widehat{a}-K)
.
∎

We can trade the divisibility assumption in Proposition
I.5.6
against a stronger hypothesis on
K
, the proof using
[
22
, 3.3]
instead of
[
22
, 3.1]
:

Corollary I.5.8
.

If
K
is henselian and
{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
is linearly surjective, then
\widehat{a}
is almost special over
K
.

The linearly newtonian setting

In this subsection
K
is an
\upomega
-free
r
-linearly newtonian
H
-asymptotic field,

r\geqslant 1
.
Thus
K
is

\operatorname{d}
-valued by Lemma
I.2.9
. We let
\phi
range over the elements active in
K
.
We now mimick the material in the previous two subsections. Note that for
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
and any element
\widehat{f}
in an asymptotic extension of
K
we have
A(\widehat{f})\preccurlyeq A^{\phi}\widehat{f}
, since

A(\widehat{f})=A^{\phi}(\widehat{f})
.

Lemma I.5.9
.

Assume that
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
has order
\leqslant r
,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
,
\gamma>0
,
{\widehat{f}\in K^{\times}}
, and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant v(A^{\phi}\widehat{f})+\gamma
, eventually. Then there exists an
f\in K
with
{A(f)=0}
and
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
.

Proof.

Take
\phi
such that
v\phi\geqslant\gamma^{\dagger}
and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant v(A^{\phi}\widehat{f})+\gamma
.
Next, take
\beta\in\Gamma
such that
\beta\geqslant\gamma
and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant v(A^{\phi}\widehat{f})+\beta
.
Then
v\phi\geqslant\beta^{\dagger}
, so
\beta>\Gamma_{\phi}^{\flat}
, hence the valuation ring of the flattening
(K^{\phi},v_{\phi}^{\flat})
is
r
-linearly surjective, by [ADH, 14.2.1].
We now apply
Lemma
I.5.2
to

(K^{\phi},v_{\phi}^{\flat}),\quad A^{\phi},\quad\dot{\beta}:=\beta+\Gamma_{\phi}^{\flat}

in the role of
K
,
A
,
\gamma
to give
f\in K
with

A(f)=0
and
v_{\phi}^{\flat}(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v_{\phi}^{\flat}(\widehat{f})+\dot{\beta}+o(\dot{\beta})
.
Then also
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\beta+o(\beta)
, and thus
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
.
∎

Lemma I.5.10
.

Assume
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
has order
\leqslant r
,
\widehat{K}
is an immediate
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic asymptotic extension of
K
,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
,
\gamma>0
,
\widehat{f}\in\widehat{K}^{\times}
, and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})\big)\geqslant v(A^{\phi}\widehat{f})+\gamma
eventually. Then
A(f)=0
and
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
for some
f\in K
.

Proof.

Since
K
is
\upomega
-free, so is
\widehat{K}
by Theorem
I.3.1
. By (
0.9
) we can extend
\widehat{K}
to arrange that

\widehat{K}
is also newtonian. Then by Lemma
I.5.9
with
\widehat{K}
in the role of
K
we get
f\in\widehat{K}
with
A(f)=0
and
v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant v(\widehat{f})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Now use that

f\in K
by [ADH, line before 14.2.10].
∎

Lemma I.5.11
.

Assume
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
has order
\leqslant r
,
b\in K
,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{Q}}\Gamma
,
\gamma>0
,
\widehat{K}
is an immediate
\operatorname{d}
-algebraic asymptotic extension of
K
, and
\widehat{f}\in\widehat{K}
,
v(\widehat{f})\geqslant o(\gamma)
. Assume also that
eventually
v(b)\geqslant v(A^{\phi})+o(\gamma)
and
v\big(A(\widehat{f})-b\big)\geqslant v(A^{\phi})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
.
Then for some
f\in K
we have
A(f)=b
and

v(\widehat{f}-f)\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
.

Proof.

We take
y\in K
with
A(y)=b
as follows: If
b=0
, then
y:=0
. If
b\neq 0
, then Corollary
I.4.7
yields
y\in K^{\times}
such that

A(y)=b
,
vy\notin{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
, and
{v_{A}^{\operatorname{e}}(vy)=vb}
. In any case,
vy\geqslant o(\gamma)
: when
b\neq 0
, the sentence preceding
[ADH, 14.2.7] gives
v_{A^{\phi}}(vy)=vb
, eventually,
to which we apply [ADH, 6.1.3].

Now
A(\widehat{g})=A(\widehat{f})-b
for
\widehat{g}:=\widehat{f}-y
, so

v(\widehat{g})\geqslant o(\gamma)
, and eventually
v\big(A(\widehat{g})\big)\geqslant v(A^{\phi})+\gamma+o(\gamma)
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)
v(\widehat{g})\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Then
v(\widehat{f}-y)\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
, so
f:=y
works.

(2)
v(\widehat{g})<(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Then by [ADH, 6.1.3] we have eventually

v(A^{\phi}\widehat{g})\ <\ v(A^{\phi})+(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma),\qquad v\big(A(\widehat{g})\big)\ \geqslant\ v(A^{\phi})+\gamma+o(\gamma),

so

v(A(\widehat{g}))\geqslant v(A^{\phi}\widehat{g})+(1/2)\gamma
, eventually.
Lemma
I.5.10

gives an element
{g\in K}
with
A(g)=0
and

v(\widehat{g}-g)\geqslant(1/2)\gamma+o(\gamma)
. Hence
f:=y+g
works.
∎

Proposition I.5.12
.

Suppose that

P\in K\{Y\}
,
