u_{i}\notin u_{j}C_{F}^{\times}K^{\times}
for
i\neq j
. Then
\sum_{i}u_{i}\notin I
.

Proof.

Suppose
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
is a counterexample with minimal
n\geqslant 1
.
Then
n\geqslant 2
and
\sum_{i}u_{i}^{\prime}\in I
, so

\sum_{i}u_{i}^{\prime}-u_{1}^{\dagger}\sum_{i}u_{i}\ =\ \sum_{i>1}(u_{i}/u_{1})^{\dagger}u_{i}\in I.

Hence

(u_{i}/u_{1})^{\dagger}=0
and thus
u_{i}/u_{1}\in C_{F}^{\times}
, for all
i>1
, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary II.2.2
.

Suppose
K
is a field and
F=K(E)
is a differential field extension of
K
with
C_{F}=C
, where
E
is a subgroup of
F^{\times}
whose elements are exponential over
K
. Then
\{y\in F^{\times}:\,\text{$y$ is exponential over}~
K
\}=K^{\times}E
.

Proof.

Let
y\in F^{\times}
be exponential over
K
. Take
K
-linearly independent
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
in
E

and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in K
with
b_{j}\neq 0
for some
j
, such that

y\ =\ \Big(\textstyle\sum_{i}a_{i}u_{i}\Big)\Big/\left(\textstyle\sum_{j}b_{j}u_{j}\right).

Then
\sum_{j}b_{j}yu_{j}-\sum_{i}a_{i}u_{i}=0
, and so Lemma
II.2.1
applied with
R=F
,
I=\{0\}
gives
b_{j}yu_{j}\in a_{i}u_{i}K^{\times}

for some
i
,
j
with
a_{i},b_{j}\neq 0
, and thus
y\in K^{\times}E
.
∎

Remark
.

In the context of Corollary
II.2.2
, see
[
23
, Theorem 1]
for the structure of the group of elements
of
F^{\times}
exponential over
K
, for finitely generated
E
.

Lemma II.2.3
.

Suppose
C_{R}^{\times}
is divisible and
E
is a subgroup of
R^{\times}
containing
C_{R}^{\times}
.
Then there is a group morphism
e\colon E^{\dagger}\to E
such that
e(b)^{\dagger}=b

for all
b\in E^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

We have a short exact sequence of commutative groups

1\to C_{R}^{\times}\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }E\xrightarrow{\ \ell\ }E^{\dagger}\to 0,

where
\iota
is the natural inclusion and
\ell(a):=a^{\dagger}
for
a\in E
. Since
C_{R}^{\times}
is divisible, this sequence splits, which is what we claimed.
∎

Let
E
,
e
,
R
be as in the previous lemma. Then
e
is injective, and its image is a complement of
C_{R}^{\times}
in
E
.
Moreover, given also a group morphism
\widetilde{e}\colon E^{\dagger}\to E
such that
\widetilde{e}(b)^{\dagger}=b
for all
b\in E^{\dagger}
,
the map
b\mapsto e(b)\widetilde{e}(b)^{-1}
is a group morphism
E^{\dagger}\to C_{R}^{\times}
.

In the rest of this section
K
is a differential field with algebraically closed constant field
C
and divisible
group
K^{\dagger}
of logarithmic derivatives.
(These conditions are satisfied if
K
is an algebraically closed differential field.) In the next subsection we show that up to isomorphism over
K
there is a unique exponential extension
R
of
K
satisfying
C_{R}=C
and
{(R^{\times})^{\dagger}=K}
. By Lemma
II.2.3
we must then have a group embedding
e\colon K\to R^{\times}
such that
e(b)^{\dagger}=b

for all
b\in K
; this motivates the construction below.

The universal exponential extension

We first describe a certain exponential extension of
K
.
For this,
take a
complement

\Lambda
of
K^{\dagger}
, that is, a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspace of
K
such that
K=K^{\dagger}\oplus\Lambda
(internal direct sum of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspaces of
K
). Below
\lambda
ranges over
\Lambda
.
Let
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
be a multiplicatively written abelian group,
isomorphic to the additive subgroup
\Lambda
of
K
,
with isomorphism
\lambda\mapsto\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\colon\Lambda\to\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
.
Put

{\operatorname{U}}\ :=\ K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big],

the group ring of
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
over
K
, an integral domain.
As
K
-linear space,

{\operatorname{U}}\ =\ \bigoplus_{\lambda}K\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\qquad(\text{an internal direct sum of $K$-linear subspaces}).

For every
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
we have a unique family
(f_{\lambda})
in
K
such that

f\ =\ \sum_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}\operatorname{e}(\lambda),

with
f_{\lambda}=0
for all but finitely many
\lambda
; we call
(f_{\lambda})
the
spectral decomposition
of
f
(with respect to
\Lambda
).
We turn
{\operatorname{U}}
into a differential ring extension of
K

by

\operatorname{e}(\lambda)^{\prime}\ =\ \lambda\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\qquad\text{for all $\lambda$.}

(Think of
\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
as
\exp(\int\lambda)
.) Thus for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
with spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda})
,

f^{\prime}\ =\ \sum_{\lambda}\big(f_{\lambda}^{\prime}+\lambda f_{\lambda}\big)\operatorname{e}(\lambda),

so
f^{\prime}
has spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda}^{\prime}+\lambda f_{\lambda})
.
Note that
{\operatorname{U}}
is exponential over
K
by Lemma
II.1.1
:
{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}=K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
, so
({\operatorname{U}}^{\times})^{\dagger}=K^{\dagger}+\Lambda=K
.

Example II.2.4
.

Let
K=C(\!(t^{{\mathbb{Q}}})\!)
be as in Example
I.2.12
, so
K^{\dagger}=({\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\smallo)t
. Take a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspace
\Lambda_{\operatorname{c}}
of
C
with
C={\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\Lambda_{\operatorname{c}}

(internal direct sum of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspaces of
C
), and let

K_{\succ}\ :=\ \big\{f\in K:\ \operatorname{supp}(f)\succ 1\big\},

a
C
-linear subspace of
K
.
Then
\Lambda:=(K_{\succ}\oplus\Lambda_{\operatorname{c}})t
is a complement to
K^{\dagger}
, and
hence
t^{-1}\Lambda=K_{\succ}\oplus\Lambda_{\operatorname{c}}
is a complement to
(K^{t})^{\dagger}
in
K^{t}
.
Moreover, if
L:=\operatorname{P}(C)\subseteq K
is the differential field of Puiseux series over
C

and
L_{\succ}:=K_{\succ}\cap L
, then
L_{\succ}\oplus\Lambda_{\operatorname{c}}
is a complement to
(L^{t})^{\dagger}
.

A subgroup
\Lambda_{0}
of
\Lambda
yields a differential subring
K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]
of
{\operatorname{U}}
that is exponential over
K
as well. These differential subrings have a useful property.
Recall from [ADH, 4.6] that a differential ring is said to be
simple
if
\{0\}
is its only proper differential ideal.

Lemma II.2.5
.

Let
\Lambda_{0}
be a subgroup of
\Lambda
. Then the differential subring

K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]
of
{\operatorname{U}}
is simple. In particular, the differential ring
{\operatorname{U}}
is simple.

Proof.

Let
I\neq R
be a differential ideal of

R:=K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]
. Let

f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in K^{\times}
and let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\Lambda_{0}
be distinct
such that
f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{i})\in I
.
If
n\geqslant 1
, then Lemma
II.2.1
yields
i\neq j

with
\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{i})/\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{j})=cg
for some constant
c
in the differential fraction field of
{\operatorname{U}}
and
some
g\in K^{\times}
, so by taking logarithmic derivatives,
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}\in K^{\dagger}
and thus
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
,
a contradiction. Thus
f=0
.
∎

Corollary II.2.6
.

Any morphism

K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]\to R
of differential
K
-algebras, with
\Lambda_{0}
a subgroup of
\Lambda
and

R
a differential ring extension of
K
, is injective.

The differential ring
{\operatorname{U}}
is the directed union of its differential subrings of the form
{\operatorname{U}}_{0}=K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]
where
\Lambda_{0}
is a finitely generated subgroup of
\Lambda
. These
{\operatorname{U}}_{0}
are simple by Lemma
II.2.5
and finitely generated as a
K
-algebra, hence their differential fraction fields have constant field
C
by [ADH, 4.6.12]. Thus the differential fraction field of
{\operatorname{U}}
has constant field
C
.

Lemma II.2.7
.

Suppose
R
is an exponential extension of
K
and

R_{0}
is a differential subring of
R
with
C_{R}^{\times}\subseteq C_{R_{0}}
and
K\subseteq(R_{0}^{\times})^{\dagger}
.
Then
R_{0}=R
.

Proof.

Let
E
be the group of units of
R
that are exponential over
K
; so
R=K[E]
.
Given
u\in E
we have
u^{\dagger}\in K\subseteq(R_{0}^{\times})^{\dagger}
, hence we have
u_{0}\in R_{0}^{\times}
with
u^{\dagger}=u_{0}^{\dagger}
, so
u=cu_{0}
with
c\in C_{R}^{\times}\subseteq C_{R_{0}}
. Thus
E\subseteq R_{0}
and so
R_{0}=R
.
∎

Corollary II.2.8
.

Every endomorphism of the differential
K
-algebra
{\operatorname{U}}
is an automorphism.

Proof.

Injectivity holds by Corollary
II.2.6
, and surjectivity by Lemma
II.2.7
.
∎

Every exponential extension of
K
with constant field
C
embeds into
{\operatorname{U}}
, and hence is an integral domain. More precisely:

Lemma II.2.9
.

Let
R
be an exponential extension of
K
such that
C_{R}^{\times}
is divisible, and set
\Lambda_{0}:=\Lambda\cap(R^{\times})^{\dagger}
, a subgroup of
\Lambda
.
Then there exists a morphism
K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]\to R
of differential
K
-algebras. Any such morphism is injective, and if
C_{R}=C
, then any such
morphism is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
E
be as in the proof of Lemma
II.2.7
, and let
e_{E}\colon E^{\dagger}\to E
be the map
e
from Lemma
II.2.3
. Since
E^{\dagger}=K^{\dagger}+\Lambda_{0}

we have

(II.2.1)

E\ =\ C_{R}^{\times}\,e_{E}(E^{\dagger})\ =\ C_{R}^{\times}\,e_{E}(K^{\dagger})\,e_{E}(\Lambda_{0})\ =\ C_{R}^{\times}\,K^{\times}\,e_{E}(\Lambda_{0}).

The group morphism
\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{0})\mapsto e_{E}(\lambda_{0})\colon\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\to E
(
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda_{0}
) extends uniquely to a
K
-algebra morphism

\iota\colon K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{0})\big]\to R=K[E]
. One verifies easily that
\iota
is a differential ring morphism. The injectivity claim follows from Corollary
II.2.6
.
If
C_{R}=C
, then
E=K^{\times}e_{E}(\Lambda_{0})
by (
II.2.1
), whence surjectivity.
∎

Recall that
{\operatorname{U}}
is an exponential extension of
K
with
C_{{\operatorname{U}}}=C

and
({\operatorname{U}}^{\times})^{\dagger}=K
. By Lemma
II.2.9
, this property characterizes
{\operatorname{U}}
up to isomorphism:

Corollary II.2.10
.

If
U
is an exponential extension of
K
such that
C_{U}=C

and
K\subseteq(U^{\times})^{\dagger}
, then
U
is isomorphic to
{\operatorname{U}}

as a differential
K
-algebra.

Now
{\operatorname{U}}
is also an exponential extension of
K
with
C_{{\operatorname{U}}}=C

and with the property that every exponential extension
R
of
K
with
C_{R}=C
embeds into
{\operatorname{U}}
as a differential
K
-algebra. This property
determines
{\operatorname{U}}
up to isomorphism as well:

Corollary II.2.11
.

Suppose
U
is an exponential extension of
K
with
C_{U}=C
such that every exponential extension
R
of
K
with
C_{R}=C
embeds into
U
as a differential
K
-algebra. Then
U
is isomorphic to
{\operatorname{U}}

as a differential
K
-algebra.

Proof.

Any embedding
{\operatorname{U}}\to U
of
differential
K
-algebras gives
K\subseteq(U^{\times})^{\dagger}
.
∎

The results above show to what extent
{\operatorname{U}}
is independent of the choice of
\Lambda
. We call
{\operatorname{U}}
the
universal exponential extension of
K
. If we need to indicate the dependence of
{\operatorname{U}}
on
K
we denote it by
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}
.
By [ADH, 5.1.40] every
{y\in{\operatorname{U}}=K\{\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\}}
satisfies a linear differential equation
A(y)=0
where
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
; in the next section we isolate conditions on
K
which ensure that every
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
has a zero
y\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}=K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
.

Corollary
II.2.10
gives for
\phi\in K^{\times}
an isomorphism

{\operatorname{U}}_{K^{\phi}}\cong({\operatorname{U}}_{K})^{\phi}
of differential
K^{\phi}
-algebras. Next we investigate how
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}
behaves when passing from
K
to a differential field extension. Therefore,

in the rest of this subsection
L
is a differential field extension of
K
with algebraically closed constant field
C_{L}
, and
L^{\dagger}
is divisible.
The next lemma relates the universal exponential extension
{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
of
L
to
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}
:

Lemma II.2.12
.

The inclusion
K\to L
extends to an embedding
\iota\colon{\operatorname{U}}_{K}\to{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
of differential rings. The image of any such embedding
\iota
is contained in
K[E]

where
E:=\{u\in{\operatorname{U}}_{L}^{\times}:u^{\dagger}\in K\}
, and if
C_{L}=C
, then
\iota({\operatorname{U}}_{K})=K[E]
.

Proof.

The differential subring

R:=K[E]
of
{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
is exponential over
K
with
(R^{\times})^{\dagger}=K
, hence Lemma
II.2.9
gives an embedding
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}\to R
of differential
K
-algebras.
Let
\iota\colon{\operatorname{U}}_{K}\to{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
be any embedding of differential
K
-algebras. Then
\iota\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big)\subseteq E
, so
\iota({\operatorname{U}}_{K})\subseteq R
; if
C_{L}=C
, then
\iota({\operatorname{U}}_{K})=R

by Lemma
II.2.7
.
∎

Corollary II.2.13
.

If
L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
and
\iota\colon{\operatorname{U}}_{K}\to{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
is an embedding of differential
K
-algebras,
then
L^{\times}\cap\iota({\operatorname{U}}_{K}^{\times})=K^{\times}
.

Proof.

Assume
L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
and identify
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}
with a differential
K
-subalgebra of
{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
via an embedding
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}\to{\operatorname{U}}_{L}
of differential
K
-algebras.
Let
a\in L^{\times}\cap{\operatorname{U}}_{K}^{\times}
; then
a^{\dagger}\in L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
, so
a=bc
where
c\in C_{L}^{\times}
,
b\in K^{\times}
.
Now
c=a/b\in C_{L}^{\times}\cap{\operatorname{U}}_{K}^{\times}=C^{\times}
, since
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}
has ring of constants
C
. So
a\in K^{\times}
as required.
∎

Suppose
L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
. Then the subspace
L^{\dagger}
of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear space
L
has a complement

\Lambda_{L}\supseteq\Lambda
. We fix such
\Lambda_{L}
and
extend
\operatorname{e}\colon\Lambda\to\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
to a group isomorphism
\Lambda_{L}\to\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{L})
, also denoted by
\operatorname{e}
, with
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{L})
a multiplicatively written commutative group extending
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
.
Let
{\operatorname{U}}_{L}:=L\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda_{L})\big]
be the corresponding universal exponential extension of
L
.
Then the natural inclusion
{\operatorname{U}}_{K}\to{\operatorname{U}}_{L}

is an embedding of differential
K
-algebras.

Automorphisms of
{\operatorname{U}}

These are easy to describe: the beginning of Section
II.1
gives a group embedding

\chi\mapsto\sigma_{\chi}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,K^{\times})\to\operatorname{Aut}\!\big(K[\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)]|K\big)

into the group of
K
-algebra automorphisms of
K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big]
, given by

\sigma_{\chi}(f)\ :=f_{\chi}\ =\ \sum_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}\chi(\lambda)\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\qquad(\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,K^{\times}),\ f\in K[\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)]).

It is easy to check that if
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,C^{\times})\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,K^{\times})
, then
\sigma_{\chi}\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
, that is,
\sigma_{\chi}
is a differential
K
-algebra automorphism of
{\operatorname{U}}
. Moreover:

Lemma II.2.14
.

The map
\chi\mapsto\sigma_{\chi}\colon\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,C^{\times})\to\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
is a group isomorphism. Its inverse assigns to
any
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
the function

\chi\colon\Lambda\to C^{\times}
given by
\chi(\lambda):=\sigma\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)\operatorname{e}(-\lambda)
. In particular,
\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
is commutative.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
and let
\chi\colon\Lambda\to{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
be given by
\chi(\lambda):=\sigma\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)\operatorname{e}(-\lambda)
.
Then
\chi(\lambda)^{\dagger}=0
for all
\lambda
. It follows easily that
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,C^{\times})
and
\sigma_{\chi}=\sigma
.
∎

The proof of the next result uses that the additive group
{\mathbb{Q}}
embeds into
C^{\times}
.

Corollary II.2.15
.

If
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
and
\sigma(f)=f
for all
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
, then
f\in K
.

Proof.

Suppose
f\in U
and
\sigma(f)=f
for all
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
.
For
\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,C^{\times})
we have
f_{\chi}=f
, that is,
f_{\lambda}\chi(\lambda)=f_{\lambda}

for all
\lambda
, so
\chi(\lambda)=1
whenever
f_{\lambda}\neq 0
. Now use that for
\lambda\neq 0
there exists

\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,C^{\times})
such that
\chi(\lambda)\neq 1
, so
f_{\lambda}=0
.
∎

Corollary II.2.16
.

Every automorphism of the differential field
K
extends to an automorphism of the differential ring
{\operatorname{U}}
.

Proof.

Lemma
II.2.3
yields a group morphism
\mu\colon K\to{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
such that
\mu(a)^{\dagger}=a
for all
a\in K
.
Let
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}(K)
. Then
\sigma
extends to an endomorphism, denoted also by
\sigma
,
of the ring
{\operatorname{U}}
, such that
\sigma\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)=\mu\big(\sigma(\lambda)\big)
for all
\lambda
.
Then

\sigma\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)^{\prime}\big)\ =\ \sigma\big(\lambda\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)\ =\ \sigma(\lambda)\mu\big(\sigma(\lambda)\big)\ =\ \mu\big(\sigma(\lambda)\big)^{\prime}\ =\ \sigma\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)^{\prime},

hence
\sigma
is an endomorphism of the differential ring
{\operatorname{U}}
.
By Lemma
II.2.5
,
\sigma
is injective, and by Lemma
II.2.7
,
\sigma
is surjective.
∎

The real case

In this subsection
K=H[\imag]
where
H
is a real closed differential subfield of
K
and
\imag^{2}=-1
.

Set
S_{C}:=\big\{c\in C:\,|c|=1\big\}
, a subgroup of
C^{\times}
. Then by Lemmas
II.1.7
and
II.2.14
:

Corollary II.2.17
.

For
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
we have the equivalence

\sigma(f^{*})=\sigma(f)^{*}\text{ for all $f\in{\operatorname{U}}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sigma=\sigma_{\chi}\text{ for some $\chi\in\operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda,S_{C})$.}

Corollaries
II.2.17
and
II.1.8
together give:

Corollary II.2.18
.

Let
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|K)
satisfy
\sigma(f^{*})=\sigma(f)^{*}
for all
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
. Then
\big\langle\sigma(f),\sigma(g)\big\rangle=\langle f,g\rangle
for all
f,g\in{\operatorname{U}}
, hence
\lVert\sigma(f)\rVert=\lVert f\rVert
for all
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
.

Next we consider the subgroup

S\ :=\ \{a+b\imag:\ a,b\in H,\ a^{2}+b^{2}=1\}

of
K^{\times}
, which is divisible,
hence so is the subgroup
S^{\dagger}
of
K^{\dagger}
.
Lemma
I.2.4
yields
K^{\dagger}=H^{\dagger}\oplus S^{\dagger}

(internal direct sum of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspaces of
K
) and
S^{\dagger}\subseteq H\imag
.
Thus we can (and do) take the complement
\Lambda
of
K^{\dagger}
in
K
so that

\Lambda=\Lambda_{\operatorname{r}}+\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}\imag
where
\Lambda_{\operatorname{r}},\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}
are subspaces of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear space
H
with
\Lambda_{\operatorname{r}}
a complement of
H^{\dagger}
in
H
and

\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}\imag

a complement of
S^{\dagger}
in
H\imag
.
The automorphism
a+b\imag\mapsto\overline{a+b\imag}:=a-b\imag
(
{a,b\in H}
) of the differential field
K
now satisfies in
{\operatorname{U}}=K[\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)]
the identity

\operatorname{e}(\overline{\lambda+\mu})\ =\ \operatorname{e}(\overline{\lambda})\operatorname{e}(\overline{\mu})\qquad(\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda),

so it extends to
an automorphism
f\mapsto\overline{f}
of the ring
{\operatorname{U}}
as follows:
for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
with spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda})
, set

\overline{f}\ :=\ \sum_{\lambda}\overline{f_{\lambda}}\operatorname{e}(\overline{\lambda})\ =\ \sum_{\lambda}\overline{f_{\overline{\lambda}}}\operatorname{e}(\lambda),

so
\overline{\operatorname{e}(\lambda)}=\operatorname{e}(\overline{\lambda})
, and
\overline{f}
has spectral decomposition
(\overline{f_{\overline{\lambda}}})
.
We have
\overline{\overline{f}}=f
for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
,
and
f\mapsto\overline{f}
lies in
\operatorname{Aut}_{\der}({\operatorname{U}}|H)
.
If
H^{\dagger}=H
, then
\Lambda_{r}=\{0\}
and hence
\overline{f}=f^{*}
for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
, where
f^{*}
is as defined in Section
II.1
.
For
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
we set

\operatorname{Re}f\ :=\ \textstyle\frac{1}{2}(f+\overline{f}),\qquad\operatorname{Im}f\ :=\ \textstyle\frac{1}{2\imag}(f-\overline{f}).

(For
f\in K
these agree with the usual real and imaginary parts of
f
as an element of
H[\imag]
.)
Consider the differential
H
-subalgebra

{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}\ :=\ \big\{f\in{\operatorname{U}}:\overline{f}=f\big\}

of
{\operatorname{U}}
.
For
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
with spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda})
we have
f\in{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}
iff
f_{\overline{\lambda}}=\overline{f_{\lambda}}
for all
\lambda
;
in particular
{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}\cap K=H
.
For
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
we have
f=(\operatorname{Re}f)+(\operatorname{Im}f)\imag
with
\operatorname{Re}f,\operatorname{Im}f\in{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}
, hence

{\operatorname{U}}\ =\ {\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}\oplus{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}\imag\quad\text{ (internal direct sum of $H$-linear subspaces).}

Let
D
be a subfield of
H
(not necessarily the constant field of
H
), so
D[\imag]
is a subfield of
K
. Let

V
be a
D[\imag]
-linear subspace of
{\operatorname{U}}
; then
V_{\operatorname{r}}:=V\cap{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}
is a
D
-linear subspace of
V
.
If
\overline{V}=V
(that is,
V
is closed under
f\mapsto\overline{f}
), then
\operatorname{Re}f,\operatorname{Im}f\in V_{\operatorname{r}}
for all
f\in V
, hence

V=V_{\operatorname{r}}\oplus V_{\operatorname{r}}\imag
(internal direct sum of
D
-linear subspaces of
V
), so
any basis of the
D
-linear space
V_{\operatorname{r}}
is a basis of the
D[\imag]
-linear space
V
.

Suppose now that
V=\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}

(internal direct sum of subspaces of
V
) where
V_{\lambda}
is for each
\lambda

a
D[\imag]
-linear subspace of
K\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
. Then
\overline{V}=V
iff

V_{\overline{\lambda}}=\overline{V_{\lambda}}
for all
\lambda
. Moreover:

Lemma II.2.19
.

Assume
H=H^{\dagger}
,
V_{0}=\{0\}
,
and
\overline{V}=V
. Let
\mathcal{V}\subseteq{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
be a basis of the subspace
\sum_{\operatorname{Im}\lambda>0}V_{\lambda}
of
V
.
Then the maps
v\mapsto\operatorname{Re}v,\ v\mapsto\operatorname{Im}v\colon\mathcal{V}\to V_{\operatorname{r}}
are injective,
\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{V}
and
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{V}
are disjoint, and
\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{V}\cup\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{V}
is
a basis of
V_{\operatorname{r}}
.

Proof.

Note that
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}\imag
. Let
\mu
range over
\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}^{>}
and set
\cal{V}_{\mu}=\cal{V}\cap K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\mu\imag)
, a basis of the
D[\imag]
-linear space
V_{\mu\imag}
. Then
\cal{V}=\bigcup_{\mu}\cal{V}_{\mu}
, a disjoint union. For
v\in\cal{V}_{\mu}
we have
v=a\operatorname{e}(\mu\imag)
with
a=a_{v}\in K^{\times}
, so

\operatorname{Re}v\ =\ \textstyle\frac{a}{2}\operatorname{e}(\mu\imag)+\textstyle\frac{\overline{a}}{2}\operatorname{e}(-\mu\imag),\qquad\operatorname{Im}v\ =\ \textstyle\frac{a}{2i}\operatorname{e}(\mu\imag)-\textstyle\frac{\overline{a}}{2\imag}\operatorname{e}(-\mu\imag),

from which it is clear that the two maps
\cal{V}\to V_{\operatorname{r}}
in the statement of the lemma are injective. It is also easy to check that
\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{V}
and
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{V}
are disjoint.

As
\mathcal{V}
is a basis of the
D[\imag]
-linear space
\sum_{\mu}V_{\mu\imag}=\sum_{\operatorname{Im}\lambda>0}V_{\lambda}
, its set of conjugates
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is a basis of the
D[\imag]
-linear space
\sum_{\mu}\overline{V_{\mu\imag}}=\sum_{\mu}V_{-\mu\imag}=\sum_{\operatorname{Im}\lambda<0}V_{\lambda}
, and so
\mathcal{V}\cup\overline{\mathcal{V}}
(a disjoint union) is a basis of
V
. Thus
\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{V}\cup\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{V}
is a basis of
V
as well. As
\operatorname{Re}\mathcal{V}\cup\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{V}
is contained in
V_{\operatorname{r}}
, it is a basis of the
D
-linear space
V_{\operatorname{r}}
.
∎

If
H=H^{\dagger}
, then
V:=\sum_{\lambda\neq 0}K\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
gives
\overline{V}=V
, so Lemma
II.2.19
gives then for
D:=H
the basis of the
H
-linear space
V_{\operatorname{r}}
consisting of the elements

\operatorname{Re}\!\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)\ =\ \textstyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)+\operatorname{e}(\overline{\lambda})\big),\qquad\operatorname{Im}\!\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)\ =\ \textstyle\frac{1}{2\imag}\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)-\operatorname{e}(\overline{\lambda})\big)\qquad(\operatorname{Im}\lambda>0).

Corollary II.2.20
.

Suppose
H=H^{\dagger}
. Set
\operatorname{c}(\lambda):=\operatorname{Re}\!\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)
and
\operatorname{s}(\lambda):=\operatorname{Im}\!\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)
,
for
\operatorname{Im}\lambda>0
. Then for
V:=\sum_{\lambda\neq 0}K\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
we have
{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}=H+V_{\operatorname{r}}
, so

{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}\ =\ H\oplus\bigoplus_{\operatorname{Im}\lambda>0}\big(H\operatorname{c}(\lambda)\oplus H\operatorname{s}(\lambda)\big)\quad\text{{(}internal direct sum of $H$-linear subspaces{)},}

and thus
{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}=H\big[\operatorname{c}(\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}^{>}\imag)\cup\operatorname{s}(\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}^{>}\imag)\big]
.

II.3.
The Spectrum of a Differential Operator

In this section
K
is a differential field,
a
,
b
range over
K
, and
A
,
B
over
K[\der]
.

This and the next two sections are mainly differential-algebraic in nature, and deal with splittings of linear differential operators.
In the present section we introduce the concept of
eigenvalue
of
A
and the
spectrum
of
A
(the collection of its eigenvalues).
In Section
II.4
we show how the eigenvalues of
A
relate to the behavior of
A
over the universal exponential extension of
K
.

Twisting

Let
L
be a differential field extension of
K
with
L^{\dagger}\supseteq K
.
Let
u\in L^{\times}
be such that
u^{\dagger}=a\in K
. Then the twist

A_{\ltimes u}=u^{-1}Au

of
A
by
u
has the same order as
A
and coefficients in
K
[ADH, 5.8.8], and only depends on
a
, not on
u
or
L
;
in fact,
\operatorname{Ri}(A_{\ltimes u})=\operatorname{Ri}(A)_{+a}
[ADH, 5.8.5].
Hence for each
a
we may define

A_{a}\ :=\ A_{\ltimes u}\ =\ u^{-1}Au\in K[\der]

where
u\in L^{\times}
is arbitrary with
u^{\dagger}=a
.
The map

A\mapsto A_{\ltimes u}
is an automorphism of the ring
K[\der]
that is
the identity on
K
(with inverse
B\mapsto B_{\ltimes u^{-1}}
);
so
A\mapsto A_{a}
is an automorphism of the ring
K[\der]
that is the identity on
K
(with inverse
B\mapsto B_{-a}
).
Note that
\der_{a}=\der+a
, and that

(a,A)\mapsto A_{a}\ :\ K\times K[\der]\to K[\der]

is an action of the additive group of
K
on the set
K[\der]
, in particular,
A_{a}=A
for
a=0
.
For
b\neq 0
we have
(A_{a})_{\ltimes b}=A_{a+b^{\dagger}}
.

Eigenvalues

In the rest of this section
A\neq 0
and
r:=\operatorname{order}(A)
.

We call

\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A)\ :=\ \dim_{C}\ker_{K}A_{a}\in\{0,\dots,r\}

the
multiplicity
of
A
at
a
.
If
B\neq 0
, then

\operatorname{mult}_{a}(B)\leqslant\operatorname{mult}_{a}(AB)
, as well as

(II.3.1)

\operatorname{mult}_{a}(AB)\ \leqslant\ \operatorname{mult}_{a}(A)+\operatorname{mult}_{a}(B),

with equality if and only if
B_{a}(K)\supseteq\ker_{K}A_{a}
; see [ADH, remarks before 5.1.12].
For
u\in K^{\times}
we have an isomorphism

y\mapsto yu\ \colon\ \ker_{K}A_{\ltimes u}\to\ker_{K}A

of
C
-linear spaces, hence

\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A)\ =\ \operatorname{mult}_{b}(A)\qquad\text{whenever $a-b\in K^{\dagger}$.}

Thus we may define the

multiplicity
of
A
at the element
[a]:=a+K^{\dagger}
of
K/K^{\dagger}

as
\operatorname{mult}_{[a]}(A):=\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A)
.

In the rest of this section
\alpha
ranges over
K/K^{\dagger}
.

We say that
\alpha
is an
eigenvalue
of
A
if
\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\geqslant 1
. Thus for
B\neq 0
: if

\alpha
is an eigenvalue of
B
of multiplicity
\mu
, then

\alpha
is an eigenvalue of
AB
of multiplicity
\geqslant\mu
;
if
\alpha
is an eigenvalue of
AB
, then it is an eigenvalue
of
A
or of
B
; and if

B_{a}(K)\supseteq\ker_{K}(A_{a})
, then

\alpha=[a]
is an eigenvalue of
AB
if and only if it is an eigenvalue
of
A
or of
B
.

Example II.3.1
.

Suppose
A=\der-a
.
Then for each element
u\neq 0
in a differential field extension of
K
with
b:=u^{\dagger}\in K
we have
A_{b}=A_{\ltimes u}=\der-(a-b)
,
so
\operatorname{mult}_{b}(A)\geqslant 1
iff
a-b\in K^{\dagger}
. Hence the only eigenvalue of
A
is
[a]
.

The
spectrum
of
A
is the set
\Sigma(A)=\Sigma_{K}(A)
of its eigenvalues. Thus
\Sigma(A)=\emptyset
if
r=0
, and
for
b\neq 0
we have
\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A)=\operatorname{mult}_{a}(bA)=\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A_{\ltimes b})
,
so
A
,
bA
, and
Ab=bA_{\ltimes b}
all have the same spectrum.
By [ADH, 5.1.21] we have

(II.3.2)

\Sigma(A)=\big\{\alpha:A\in K[\der](\der-a)\text{ for some $a$ with $[a]=\alpha$}\big\}.

Hence for irreducible
A
:
\ \Sigma(A)\neq\emptyset\ \Leftrightarrow\ r=1
.
From (
II.3.1
) we obtain:

Lemma II.3.2
.

Suppose
B\neq 0
and set
s:=\operatorname{order}B
. Then

\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(B)\ \leqslant\ \operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(AB)\ \leqslant\ \operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)+\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(B),

where the second inequality is an equality if
K
is
s
-linearly surjective. Hence

\Sigma(B)\ \subseteq\ \Sigma(AB)\ \subseteq\ \Sigma(A)\cup\Sigma(B).

If
K
is
s
-linearly surjective,
then
\Sigma(AB)=\Sigma(A)\cup\Sigma(B)
.

Example
.

For
n\geqslant 1
we have
\Sigma\big((\der-a)^{n}\big)=\big\{[a]\big\}
. (By induction on
n
, using Example
II.3.1
and Lemma
II.3.2
.)

It follows from Lemma
II.3.2
that
A
has at most
r
eigenvalues. More precisely:

Lemma II.3.3
.

We have
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\leqslant r
.
If
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=r
, then
A
splits over
K
; the converse holds if

r=1
or
K
is
1
-linearly surjective.

Proof.

By induction on
r
. The case
r=0
is obvious,
so suppose
r>0
. We may also assume
\Sigma(A)\neq\emptyset
: otherwise
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=0

and
A
does not split over
K
. Now (
II.3.2
) gives
a
,
B
with

A=B(\der-a)
. By Example
II.3.1
we have
\Sigma(\der-a)=\big\{[a]\big\}
and
\operatorname{mult}_{a}(\der-a)=1
.
By the inductive hypothesis applied to
B
and the second inequality in Lemma
II.3.2
we thus get
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\leqslant r
.

Suppose that
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=r
.
Then
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(B)=r-1
by
Lemma
II.3.2
and
the inductive hypothesis applied to
B
.
Therefore
B
splits over
K
, again by the inductive hypothesis, and so does
A
.
Finally, if
K
is
1
-linearly surjective and
A
splits over
K
, then we arrange that
B
splits over
K
,
so
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(B)=r-1
by the inductive hypothesis,
hence
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=r
by Lemma
II.3.2
.
∎

Section
II.4
gives a more explicit proof of Lemma
II.3.3
,
under additional hypotheses on
K
.
Next, let
L
be a differential field extension of
K
. Then

\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A)
does not strictly decrease in passing from
K
to
L
[ADH, 4.1.13]. Hence the group morphism

a+K^{\dagger}\mapsto a+L^{\dagger}\colon K/K^{\dagger}\to L/L^{\dagger}

restricts to a map

\Sigma_{K}(A)\to\Sigma_{L}(A)
; in particular, if
\Sigma_{K}(A)\neq\emptyset
, then
\Sigma_{L}(A)\neq\emptyset
.
If
L^{\dagger}\cap K=K^{\dagger}
, then
\lvert\Sigma_{K}(A)\rvert\leqslant\lvert\Sigma_{L}(A)\rvert
, and

\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)
also
does not strictly decrease if
K
is replaced by
L
.

Lemma II.3.4
.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in K
and

A\ =\ (\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{1}),\quad\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\ =\ r.

Then the spectrum of
A
is
\big\{[a_{1}],\dots,[a_{r}]\big\}
, and for all
\alpha
,

\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\ =\ \big|\big\{i\in\{1,\dots,r\}:\ \alpha=[a_{i}]\big\}\big|.

Proof.

Let
i
range over
\{1,\dots,r\}
.
By Lemma
II.3.2
and Example
II.3.1
,

\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\ \leqslant\ \sum_{i}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(\der-a_{i})\ =\ \big|\big\{i:\alpha=[a_{i}]\big\}\big|

and hence

r\ =\ \sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\ \leqslant\ \sum_{\alpha}\big|\big\{i:\alpha=[a_{i}]\big\}\big|\ =\ r.

Thus for each
\alpha
we have
\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=\big|\big\{i:\alpha=[a_{i}]\big\}\big|
as required.
∎

Recall from [ADH, 5.1.8] that
D^{*}\in K[\der]
denotes the
adjoint
of
D\in K[\der]
, and that the map
D\mapsto D^{*}
is an involution of the ring
K[\der]
with
a^{*}=a
for all
a
and
\der^{*}=-\der
.
If
A
splits over
K
, then so does
A^{*}
. Furthermore,
(A_{a})^{*}=(A^{*})_{-a}
for all
a
. By
Lemmas
II.3.3
and
II.3.4
:

Corollary II.3.5
.

Suppose
K
is
1
-linearly surjective and
\sum_{\alpha}\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=r
. Then
\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)=\operatorname{mult}_{-\alpha}(A^{*})

for all
\alpha
. In particular,
the map
\alpha\mapsto-\alpha
restricts to a bijection
\Sigma(A)\to\Sigma(A^{*})
.

Let
\phi\in K^{\times}
. Then
(A^{\phi})_{a}=(A_{\phi a})^{\phi}
and hence

\operatorname{mult}_{a}(A^{\phi})\ =\ \operatorname{mult}_{\phi a}(A),

so the group isomorphism

(II.3.3)

[a]\mapsto[\phi a]\ \colon\ K^{\phi}/\phi^{-1}K^{\dagger}\to K/K^{\dagger}

maps
\Sigma(A^{\phi})
onto
\Sigma(A)
.

Note that
K[\der]/K\der]A
as a
K
-linear space has dimension
r=\operatorname{order}A
.
Recall from [ADH, 5.1] that
A
and
B\neq 0
are said to
have the same type
if the (left)
K[\der]
-modules

K[\der]/K[\der]A
and
K[\der]/K[\der]B
are isomorphic (and so
\operatorname{order}B=r
). By [ADH, 5.1.19]:

Lemma II.3.6
.

The operators
A
and
B\neq 0
have the same type iff
\operatorname{order}B=r
and there is
R\in K[\der]
of order
<r
with
1\in K[\der]R+K[\der]A
and
BR\in K[\der]A
.

Hence if
A
,
B
have the same type, then they also have the same type as elements of
L[\der]
, for any differential field
extension
L
of
K
. Since
B\mapsto B_{a}
is an automorphism of the ring
K[\der]
, Lemma
II.3.6
and [ADH, 5.1.20] yield:

Lemma II.3.7
.

If
A
and
B\neq 0
have the same type, then so do
A_{a}
,
B_{a}
, for all
a
, and thus

A
,
B
have the same eigenvalues, with same multiplicity.

By this lemma the spectrum of
A
depends only on the type of
A
, that is, on the isomorphism type of the
K[\der]
-module

K[\der]/K[\der]A
, suggesting one might try to associate a spectrum to each differential module over
K
.
(Recall from [ADH, 5.5] that a differential module over
K
is a
K[\der]
-module of finite dimension as
K
-linear space.) We do not develop this point of view further in the present monograph, where
our focus is on linear differential operators. (There will be more on this in
[
7
]
.) But we remark here that it motivates the terminology of “eigenvalues” originating in the case of the
differential field of Puiseux series over
{\mathbb{C}}
treated in
[
20
]
.

II.4.
Eigenvalues and Splittings

In this section
K
is a differential field such that
C
is algebraically closed and
K^{\dagger}
is divisible.

We let
A
,
B
range over
K[\der]
, and we assume
A\neq 0
and set
r:=\operatorname{order}A
.

Spectral decomposition of differential operators

Fix a complement
\Lambda
of the subspace
K^{\dagger}
of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear space
K
, let
{\operatorname{U}}:=K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big]
be the
universal exponential extension of
K
, let
\Omega
be the differential fraction field of the differential
K
-algebra
{\operatorname{U}}
, and let
\lambda
range over
\Lambda
. Then

A_{\lambda}\ =\ A_{\ltimes\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)}\ =\ \operatorname{e}({-\lambda})A\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\in K[\der].

Moreover, for every
a\in K
there is a unique
\lambda
with
a-\lambda\in K^{\dagger}
, so

\operatorname{mult}_{[a]}(A)=\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)
. Call
\lambda
an
eigenvalue
of
A
with respect
to our complement
\Lambda
of
K^{\dagger}
in
K
if
[\lambda]
is an eigenvalue of
A
; thus the group isomorphism
\lambda\mapsto[\lambda]\colon\Lambda\to K/K^{\dagger}
maps the set of eigenvalues of
A
with respect to
\Lambda
onto the spectrum of
A
.
For
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
with spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda})
we have

A(f)\ =\ \sum_{\lambda}A_{\lambda}(f_{\lambda})\,\operatorname{e}(\lambda),

so
A({\operatorname{U}}^{\times})\subseteq{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}\cup\{0\}
.
We call the family
(A_{\lambda})
the
spectral decomposition
of
A
(with respect to
\Lambda
).
Given a
C
-linear subspace
V
of
{\operatorname{U}}
, we set
V_{\lambda}:=V\cap K\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
, a
C
-linear subspace of
V
; the sum
\sum_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}
is direct. For
V:={\operatorname{U}}
we have
{\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda}=K\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
, and
{\operatorname{U}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda}{\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda}

with
A({\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda})\subseteq{\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda
.
Taking
V:=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
, we obtain
V_{\lambda}=(\ker_{K}A_{\lambda})\operatorname{e}(\lambda)

and hence
\dim_{C}V_{\lambda}=\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)
,
and
V=\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}
. Thus

(II.4.1)

\lvert\Sigma(A)\rvert\ \leqslant\ \sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)\ =\ \dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A\ \leqslant\ r.

Moreover:

Lemma II.4.1
.

The
C
-linear space
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
has a basis contained in
{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}=K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
.

Example
.

We have a
C
-algebra isomorphism
P(Y)\mapsto P(\der)\colon C[Y]\to C[\der]
.
Suppose
A\in C[\der]\subseteq K[\der]
, let
P(Y)\in C[Y]
,
P(\der)=A
, and let

c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in C
be the distinct zeros of
P
, of respective multiplicities
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\geqslant 1}
(so
r=\deg P=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}
).
Suppose also
C\subseteq\Lambda
, and
x\in K
satisfies
x^{\prime}=1
.
(This holds in Example
II.2.4
.)
Then the
x^{i}\operatorname{e}(c_{j})\in{\operatorname{U}}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
,
0\leqslant i<m_{j}
)
form a basis of the
C
-linear space
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
by [ADH, 5.1.18].
So the eigenvalues of
A
with respect to
\Lambda
are
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
, with respective multiplicities
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
.

Corollary II.4.2
.

Suppose
\dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r\geqslant 1
and
A=\der^{r}+a_{r-1}\der^{r-1}+\cdots+a_{0}
where
a_{0},\dots,a_{r-1}\in K
.
Then

\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)\lambda\ \equiv\ -a_{r-1}\mod K^{\dagger}.

In particular,
\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)\lambda=0
iff
a_{r-1}\in K^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Take a basis
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}
of
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
with
y_{j}=f_{j}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{j})
,
f_{j}\in K^{\times}
,
\lambda_{j}\in\Lambda
.
The Wronskian matrix
\operatorname{Wr}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
of
(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
[ADH, p. 206] equals

\operatorname{Wr}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})\ =\ M\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{1})&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{r})\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{where $M\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(K)$.}

Then
w:=\operatorname{wr}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})=\det\operatorname{Wr}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})\neq 0
by [ADH, 4.1.13] and

-a_{r-1}\ =\ w^{\dagger}\ =\ (\det M)^{\dagger}+\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{r}

where we used [ADH, 4.1.17] for the first equality.
∎

If
A
splits over
K
, then so does
A_{\lambda}
.
Moreover, if
A_{\lambda}(K)=K
, then
A({\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda})={\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda}
:
for
f,g\in K
with
A_{\lambda}(f)=g
we have

A\big(f\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)=g\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
.
Thus:

Lemma II.4.3
.

Suppose
K
is
r
-linearly surjective, or
K
is
1
-linearly surjective and
A
splits over
K
. Then
A({\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda})={\operatorname{U}}_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda
and hence
A({\operatorname{U}})={\operatorname{U}}
.

In the next subsection we study the
connection between splittings of
A
and
bases of the
C
-linear space
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
in more detail.

Constructing splittings and bases

Recall that
\operatorname{order}A=r\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Set
{\operatorname{U}}={\operatorname{U}}_{K}
, so
{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}=K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
. Let

y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
. We construct a sequence
A_{0},\dots,A_{n}
of monic operators in
K[\der]
with
n\leqslant r
as follows.
First, set
A_{0}:=1
. Next, given
A_{0},\dots,A_{i-1}
in
K[\der]^{\neq}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
), set
f_{i}:=A_{i-1}(y_{i})
; if
f_{i}\neq 0
, then
f_{i}\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
, so
f_{i}^{\dagger}\in K
, and the next term in the sequence
is

A_{i}\ :=\ (\der-a_{i})A_{i-1},\qquad a_{i}\ :=\ f_{i}^{\dagger},

whereas if
f_{i}=0
, then
n:=i-1
and the construction is finished.

Lemma II.4.4
.

\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A_{i}=Cy_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus Cy_{i}

(
internal direct sum
)
for
i=0,\dots,n
.

Proof.

By induction on
i\leqslant n
. The case
i=0
being trivial, suppose
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and the claim holds
for
i-1
in place of
i
. Then
A_{i-1}(y_{i})=f_{i}\neq 0
, hence
y_{i}\notin\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A_{i-1}=Cy_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus Cy_{i-1}
,
and
A_{i}=(\der-f_{i}^{\dagger})A_{i-1}
, so by [ADH, 5.1.14(i)] we have

\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A_{i}=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A_{i-1}\oplus Cy_{i}=Cy_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus Cy_{i}
.
∎

We denote the tuple
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in K^{n}
just constructed by
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
,
so
A_{n}=(\der-a_{n})\cdots(\der-a_{1})
.
Suppose
r\geqslant 1
. Then
n\geqslant 1
,
a_{1}=y_{1}^{\dagger}
,
A_{1}=\der-a_{1}
,

A_{1}(y_{2}),\dots,A_{1}(y_{n})\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
, and we have

(a_{2},\dots,a_{n})\ =\ \operatorname{split}\!\big(A_{1}(y_{2}),\dots,A_{1}(y_{n})\big).

By Lemma
II.4.4
,
n\leqslant r
is maximal such that
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
are
C
-linearly independent.
In particular,
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}
are
C
-linearly independent iff
n=r
.

Corollary II.4.5
.

If
A(y_{i})=0
for
i=1,\dots,n
, then
A\in K[\der]A_{n}
.
Thus if
n=r
and
A(y_{i})=0
for
i=1,\dots,r
, then

A=a(\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{1})
where
a\in K^{\times}
.

This follows from [ADH, 5.1.15(i)] and Lemma
II.4.4
.

Suppose that
H
is a differential subfield of
K
and
y_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,y_{r}^{\dagger}\in H
. Then we have
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})\in~H^{n}
: use that
y^{\prime}\in Hy
with
y\in U
gives
y^{(m)}\in Hy
for all
m
, so
B(y)\in Hy

for all
B\in H[\der]
, hence for such
B
, if
f:=B(y)\neq 0
, then
f^{\dagger}\in H
.

Corollary II.4.6
.

Suppose
\dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
. Then
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=\ker_{\Omega}A
and
A
splits over
K
. If

A=(\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{1}),\ a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in K
,
then the spectrum of
A
is
\big\{[a_{1}],\dots,[a_{r}]\big\}
, and for all
\alpha\in K/K^{\dagger}
,

\operatorname{mult}_{\alpha}(A)\ =\ \big|\big\{i\in\{1,\dots,r\}:\ \alpha=[a_{i}]\big\}\big|.

Proof.

A
splits over
K
by
Lemma
II.4.1
and Corollary
II.4.5
.
The rest follows from Lemma
II.3.4
in view of
\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)=\dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
.
∎

Conversely, we can associate to a given splitting of
A
over
K
a basis of
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
consisting of
r
elements
of
{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
, provided
K
is
1
-linearly surjective when
r\geqslant 2
:

Lemma II.4.7
.

Assume
K
is
1
-linearly surjective in case
r\geqslant 2
. Let

A\ =\ (\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{1})\qquad\text{where $a_{i}=b_{i}^{\dagger}+\lambda_{i}$, $b_{i}\in K^{\times}$, $\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda$ {(}$i=1,\dots,r${)}.}

Then there are
C
-linearly independent
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\in\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A

with
y_{i}\in K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,r
and
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
.

Proof.

By induction on
r
. The case
r=0
is trivial, and for
r=1
we can take
y_{1}=b_{1}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{1})
. Let
r\geqslant 2
and suppose inductively that for

B\ :=\ (\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{2})

we have
C
-linearly independent
z_{2},\dots,z_{r}\in\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}B
with

z_{i}\in K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{i})
for
{i=2,\dots,r}
and
\operatorname{split}(z_{2},\dots,z_{r})=(a_{2},\dots,a_{r})
.
For
i=2,\dots,r
, Lemma
II.4.3
gives
{y_{i}\in K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{i})}
with
(\der-a_{1})(y_{i})=z_{i}
. Set
y_{1}:=b_{1}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{1})
, so
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}(\der-a_{1})=~Cy_{1}
. Then
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\in\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A

are
C
-linearly independent such that
y_{i}\in K^{\times}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{i})
for
{i=1,\dots,r}
, and one
verifies easily that
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
.
∎

Corollary II.4.8
.

Assume
K
is
1
-linearly surjective when
r\geqslant 2
. Then

\text{$A$ splits over~$K$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A\ =\ r.

Remark
.

If
\dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A=r
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}
are the eigenvalues of
A
with respect to
\Lambda
, then the differential subring
K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{1}),\operatorname{e}(-\lambda_{1}),\dots,\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{d}),\operatorname{e}(-\lambda_{d})\big]
of
{\operatorname{U}}
is the Picard-Vessiot ring for
A
over
K
; see
[
20
, Section 1.3]
.
If
K
is linearly closed and linearly surjective,
then
{\operatorname{U}}
is by Corollary
II.4.8
the universal Picard-Vessiot ring of the differential field
K
as defined in
[
20
, Chapter 10]
.
Our construction of
{\operatorname{U}}
above is modeled on the description of the universal Picard-Vessiot ring
of the algebraic closure of
C(\!(t)\!)
given in
[
20
, Chapter 3]
.

Recalling our convention that
r=\operatorname{order}A
, here is a complement to Lemma
II.4.1
:

Corollary II.4.9
.

Let
V
be a
C
-linear subspace of
{\operatorname{U}}
with
r=\dim_{C}V
. Then
there is at most one monic
A
with
V=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
. Moreover,
the following are equivalent:

(i)

V=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
for some monic
A
that splits over
K
;

(ii)

V=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}B
for some
B\neq 0
;

(iii)

V=\sum_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}
;

(iv)

V
has a basis contained in
{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from [ADH, 5.1.15] applied to the differential fraction field of
{\operatorname{U}}
in place of
K
.
The implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is clear,
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) was noted
before Lemma
II.4.1
, and (iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) is obvious.
For (iv)
\Rightarrow
(i),
let
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
be a basis of
V
. Then
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})\in K^{r}
,
so
V=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
for
A=(\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{1})
by Lemma
II.4.4
, so (i) holds.
∎

Let
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
and
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}):=\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
. We finish this subsection with some remarks about
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
for use in
[
7
]
.
Let
A_{0},\dots,A_{n}\in K[\der]
be as above and
recall that
n\leqslant r
is maximal such that
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
are
C
-linearly independent.

Lemma II.4.10
.

Assume
n=r
.
Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
are
C
-linearly independent and
(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})=\operatorname{split}(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
;

(ii)

for
i=1,\dots,r
there are
c_{ii},c_{i,i-1},\dots,c_{i1}\in C
such that

z_{i}\ =\ c_{ii}y_{i}+c_{i,i-1}y_{i-1}+\cdots+c_{i1}y_{1}\ \text{and}\ c_{ii}\neq 0.

Proof.

The case
r=0
is trivial. Let
r=1
. If (i) holds, then
y_{1}^{\dagger}=a_{1}=z_{1}^{\dagger}
, hence
z_{1}\in C^{\times}\,y_{1}
, so (ii) holds. The converse is obvious.
Let
r\geqslant 2
, and assume (i) holds. Put
\widetilde{y}_{i}:=A_{1}(y_{i})
and
\widetilde{z}_{i}:=A_{1}(z_{i})
for
i=2,\dots,r
. Then

\operatorname{split}(\widetilde{y}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{y}_{r})\ =\ (a_{2},\dots,a_{r})\ =\ \operatorname{split}(\widetilde{z}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{z}_{r}),

so we can assume inductively to have
c_{ij}\in C
(
2\leqslant j\leqslant i\leqslant r
) with

\widetilde{z}_{i}\ =\ c_{ii}\widetilde{y}_{i}+c_{i,i-1}\widetilde{y}_{i-1}+\cdots+c_{i2}\widetilde{y}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad c_{ii}\neq 0\qquad(2\leqslant i\leqslant r).

Hence for
2\leqslant i\leqslant r
,

z_{i}\in c_{ii}y_{i}+c_{i,i-1}y_{i-1}+\cdots+c_{i2}y_{2}+\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A_{1}.

Now use
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A_{1}=Cy_{1}
to conclude (ii). For the converse, let

c_{ij}\in C
be as in (ii). Then clearly
z_{1},\dots,z_{r}
are
C
-linearly independent.
Let
(b_{1},\dots,b_{r}):=\operatorname{split}(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
and
B_{r-1}:=(\der-b_{r-1})\cdots(\der-b_{1})
.
Then
a_{r}=f_{r}^{\dagger}
where
f_{r}=A_{r-1}(y_{r})\neq 0
, and
b_{r}=g_{r}^{\dagger}
where
g_{r}:=B_{r-1}(z_{r})\neq 0
.
Now inductively we have
a_{j}=b_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,r-1
, so
A_{r-1}=B_{r-1}
, and
A_{r-1}(y_{i})=0
for
i=1,\dots,r-1
by Lemma
II.4.4
.
Hence
g_{r}=c_{rr}f_{r}
, and thus
a_{r}=b_{r}
.
∎

Lemma II.4.11
.

Let
z\in{\operatorname{U}}^{\times}
. Then

\operatorname{split}(y_{1}z,\dots,y_{r}z)=(a_{1}+z^{\dagger},\dots,a_{n}+z^{\dagger})
.

Proof.

Since for
m\leqslant r
, the units
y_{1}z,\dots,y_{m}z
of
{\operatorname{U}}
are
C
-linearly independent iff
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
are

C
-linearly independent, we see that the tuples
\operatorname{split}(y_{1}z,\dots,y_{r}z)
and

\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})
have the same length
n
.
Let
(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}):=\operatorname{split}(y_{1}z,\dots,y_{r}z)
; we show
(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})=(a_{1}+z^{\dagger},\dots,a_{n}+z^{\dagger})

by induction on
n
. The case
n=0
is obvious, so suppose
n\geqslant 1
.
Then
a_{1}=y_{1}^{\dagger}
and
b_{1}=(y_{1}z)^{\dagger}=a_{1}+z^{\dagger}
as required.
By remarks following the proof of Lemma
II.4.4
we have

(a_{2},\dots,a_{n})\ =\ \operatorname{split}\!\big(A_{1}(y_{2}),\dots,A_{1}(y_{n})\big)\qquad\text{where $A_{1}:=\der-a_{1}$.}

Now
B_{1}:=\der-b_{1}=(A_{1})_{\ltimes z^{-1}}
, so likewise

(b_{2},\dots,b_{n})\ =\ \operatorname{split}\!\big(B_{1}(y_{2}z),\dots,B_{1}(y_{n}z)\big)\ =\ \operatorname{split}\!\big(A_{1}(y_{2})z,\dots,A_{1}(y_{n})z\big).

Hence
b_{2}=a_{2}+z^{\dagger},\dots,b_{n}=a_{n}+z^{\dagger}
by our inductive hypothesis.
∎

For
f\in\der K
we let
\int f
denote an element of
K
such that
(\int f)^{\prime}=f
.

Lemma II.4.12
.

Let
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}\in K^{\times}
and

A\ =\ g_{1}\cdots g_{r}(\der g_{r}^{-1})(\der g_{r-1}^{-1})\cdots(\der g_{1}^{-1}),

and suppose the integrals below can be chosen such that

y_{1}\ =\ g_{1},\quad y_{2}\ =\ g_{1}\textstyle\int g_{2},\quad\dots,\quad y_{r}\ =\ g_{1}\int(g_{2}\int g_{3}(\cdots(g_{r-1}\int g_{r})\cdots)),

Then
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\in K^{\times}
,
n=r
, and
a_{i}=(g_{1}\cdots g_{i})^{\dagger}
for
i=1,\dots,r
.

Proof.

Let
b_{i}:=(g_{1}\cdots g_{i})^{\dagger}
for
i=1,\dots,r
. By induction on
i=0,\dots,r
we show
n\geqslant i
and

(a_{1},\dots,a_{i})=(b_{1},\dots,b_{i})
. This is clear for
i=0
, so suppose
{i\in\{1,\dots,r\}}
,
n\geqslant i-1
, and
(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1})=(b_{1},\dots,b_{i-1})
.
Then

A_{i-1}=(\der-a_{i-1})\cdots(\der-a_{1})=(\der-b_{i-1})\cdots(\der-b_{1})=g_{1}\cdots g_{i-1}(\der g_{i-1}^{-1})\cdots(\der g_{1}^{-1}),

using Lemma
I.1.3
for the last equality. So
A_{i-1}(y_{i})=g_{1}\cdots g_{i}\neq 0
, and thus
n\geqslant i
and

a_{i}=A_{i-1}(y_{i})^{\dagger}=b_{i}
.
∎

The case of real operators

We now continue the subsection
The real case
of Section
II.2
.
Thus
K=H[\imag]
where
H
is a real closed differential subfield of
K
and
{\imag^{2}=-1}
, and
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\operatorname{r}}+\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}\imag
where
\Lambda_{\operatorname{r}}
,
\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}
are subspaces
of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear space
H
.
The complex conjugation automorphism
z\mapsto\overline{z}
of the differential field
K
extends uniquely to an automorphism

B\mapsto\overline{B}
of the ring
K[\der]
with
\overline{\der}=\der
. We have
\overline{A(f)}=\overline{A}(\overline{f})
for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
, from which it follows that
\dim_{C}\ker_{K}A=\dim_{C}\ker_{K}\overline{A}
,

(\overline{A})_{\lambda}=\overline{(A_{\overline{\lambda}})}
,

\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}\overline{A}=\operatorname{mult}_{\overline{\lambda}}A
, and
f\mapsto\overline{f}\colon{\operatorname{U}}\to{\operatorname{U}}
restricts to a
C_{H}
-linear bijection
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A\to\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}\overline{A}
.

In the rest of this subsection we assume
H=H^{\dagger}

(
so
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}\imag)
and
A\in H[\der]

(
and by earlier conventions,
A\neq 0
and
r:=\operatorname{order}A)
.

Then
A=\overline{A}
, hence for all
\lambda
we have
A_{\overline{\lambda}}=\overline{A_{\lambda}}
and
\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}A=\operatorname{mult}_{\overline{\lambda}}A
. Thus with
\mu
ranging over
\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}^{>}
:

\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)\ =\ \operatorname{mult}_{0}(A)+2\sum_{\mu}\operatorname{mult}_{\mu\imag}(A).

Note that
0
is an eigenvalue of
A
iff
\ker_{H}A\neq\{0\}
.

Let
V:=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
, a subspace of the
C
-linear space
{\operatorname{U}}
with
\overline{V}=V
and
\dim_{C}V\leqslant r
.
Recall that we have the differential
H
-subalgebra
{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}=\{f\in{\operatorname{U}}:\overline{f}=f\}
of
{\operatorname{U}}

and the
C_{H}
-linear subspace
V_{\operatorname{r}}=\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}}A
of
{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}
.
Now

V=V_{\operatorname{r}}\oplus V_{\operatorname{r}}\imag
(internal direct sum of
C_{H}
-linear subspaces), so

\dim_{C}V=\dim_{C_{H}}V_{\operatorname{r}}
.
Combining Lemma
II.4.1
and the remarks preceding it with Lemma
II.2.19
and its proof yields:

Corollary II.4.13
.

The
C
-linear space
V
has a basis

a_{1}\operatorname{e}(\mu_{1}\imag),\,\overline{a_{1}}\operatorname{e}(-\mu_{1}\imag),\ \dots,\ a_{m}\operatorname{e}(\mu_{m}\imag),\,\overline{a_{m}}\operatorname{e}(-\mu_{m}\imag),\ h_{1},\ \dots,\ h_{n}\qquad(2m+n\leqslant r),

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in K^{\times}
,
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m}\in\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}^{>}
,
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}\in H^{\times}
. For such a basis,

\operatorname{Re}\!\big(a_{1}\operatorname{e}(\mu_{1}\imag)\big),\,\operatorname{Im}\!\big(a_{1}\operatorname{e}(\mu_{1}\imag)\big),\ \dots,\ \operatorname{Re}\!\big(a_{m}\operatorname{e}(\mu_{m}\imag)\big),\,\operatorname{Im}\!\big(a_{m}\operatorname{e}(\mu_{m}\imag)\big),\ h_{1},\ \dots,\ h_{n}

is a basis of the
C_{H}
-linear space
V_{\operatorname{r}}
, and
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
is a basis of the
C_{H}
-linear subspace
\ker_{H}A=V\cap H
of
H
.

Using
H=H^{\dagger}
, arguments as in the proof of Lemma
II.4.7
show:

Lemma II.4.14
.

Assume
H
is
1
-linearly surjective when
r\geqslant 2
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in H
be such that

A=(\der-a_{r})\cdots(\der-a_{1})
.
Then the
C_{H}
-linear space
\ker_{H}A
has a basis
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}

such that
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
.

Recall from Lemma
II.3.3
that
if
r=1
or
K
is
1
-linearly surjective, then

\text{$A$ splits over $K$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)=r.

Now
\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)=\operatorname{mult}_{\overline{\lambda}}(A)
for all
\lambda
, so if

\operatorname{mult}_{\lambda}(A)=r\geqslant 1
, then
\lambda=0
.
Also, for
W:=V\cap K=\ker_{K}A
and
W_{\operatorname{r}}:=W\cap{\operatorname{U}}_{\operatorname{r}}
we have
W_{\operatorname{r}}=\ker_{H}A
and

W\ =\ W_{\operatorname{r}}\oplus W_{\operatorname{r}}\imag\quad\text{ (internal direct sum of $C_{H}$-linear subspaces)},

so
\operatorname{mult}_{0}(A)=\dim_{C}\ker_{K}A=\dim_{C_{H}}\ker_{H}A
. If
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}
is a basis of the
C_{H}
-linear space
\ker_{H}A
, then
\operatorname{split}(y_{1},\dots,y_{r})\in H^{r}
in reversed order is a splitting of
A
over
H
by Corollary
II.4.5
.
These remarks and Lemma
II.4.14
now yield:

Corollary II.4.15
.

If

\operatorname{mult}_{0}(A)=r
, then
A
splits over
H
. The converse
holds if
H
is
1
-linearly surjective or
r=1
.

Corollary II.4.16
.

Suppose
r\geqslant 1
, and
K
is
1
-linearly surjective if
r\geqslant 2
. Then

\text{$A$ splits over~$H$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\operatorname{mult}_{0}(A)=r\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\lvert\Sigma(A)\rvert=1.

We now focus on the order
2
case:

Lemma II.4.17
.

Suppose
r=2
and
A
splits over
K
but not over
H
.
Then

\dim_{C}\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A\ =\ 2.

If
H
is
1
-linearly surjective,
then
A
has two distinct eigenvalues.

Proof.

We can assume
A
is monic, so
A=(\der-f)(\der-g)
with
f,g\in K
and
g=a+b\imag
,
a,b\in H
,
b\neq 0
. Then
g=d^{\dagger}+\mu\imag
with
d\in K^{\times}
and
\mu\in\Lambda_{\operatorname{i}}
, and so
d\operatorname{e}(\mu\imag)\in\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A
. From
A=\overline{A}
we obtain
\overline{d}\operatorname{e}(-\mu\imag)\in\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A
. These two elements of
\ker_{{\operatorname{U}}}A
are
C
-linearly independent, since

d\operatorname{e}(\mu\imag)/\overline{d}\operatorname{e}(-\mu\imag)\ =\ (d/\overline{d})\operatorname{e}(2\mu\imag)\notin C:

this is clear if
\mu\neq 0
, and if
\mu=0
, then
d^{\dagger}=g
, so
{(d/\overline{d})^{\dagger}=g-\overline{g}=2b\imag\neq 0}
, and hence
d/\overline{d}\notin C
. Thus
\dim_{C}\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A\ =\ 2
, and
\mu\imag
,
-\mu\imag
are eigenvalues of
A
with respect to
\Lambda
. Now assume
H
is
1
-linearly surjective. Then we claim that
{\mu\neq 0}
. To see this note that [ADH, 5.1.21, 5.2.10] and the assumption that
A
does not split over
H
yield
\dim_{C_{H}}\ker_{H}A=\dim_{C}\ker_{K}A=0
, hence
g\notin K^{\dagger}
and thus
{\mu\imag=g-d^{\dagger}\neq 0}
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
II.4.14
and
II.4.17
yields:

Corollary II.4.18
.

If
H
is
1
-linearly surjective,
A
has order
2
, and
A
splits over
K
, then
\dim_{C}\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A\ =\ 2
.

In the rest of this subsection
H
is
1
-linearly surjective and
A=4\der^{2}+f
,
f\in H
.

Let
\omega\colon H\to H
and
\sigma\colon H^{\times}\to H
be as in (
0.5
) and (
0.6
). Then
by (
0.7
) and (
0.8
):

A
splits over
H

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad f\in\omega(H),

A
splits over
K

\displaystyle\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad f\in\sigma(H^{\times})\cup\omega(H).

If
A
splits over
H
, then
\Sigma(A)=\{0\}
and
\operatorname{mult}_{0}(A)=2
, by Corollary
II.4.16
.
Suppose
A
splits over
K
but not over
H
, and let
y\in H^{\times}
satisfy
\sigma(y)=f\notin\omega(H)
.
Then by [ADH, p. 262] we have
A=4(\der+g)(\der-g)
where
g=\frac{1}{2}(-y^{\dagger}+y\imag)
.
Hence the two distinct eigenvalues of
A

are
(y/2)\imag+K^{\dagger}
and
-(y/2)\imag+K^{\dagger}
.

The case of oscillating transseries

We now apply the results above to the algebraically closed differential field
K={\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
. Note that
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
has constant field
{\mathbb{C}}
and extends the (real closed) differential field
{\mathbb{T}}
of transseries.
After (
0.12
) in the introduction, we already remarked:

Lemma II.4.19
.

{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
is linearly closed and linearly surjective.

Now applying Corollary
II.4.8
and Lemma
II.4.1
to
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
gives:

Corollary II.4.20
.

For
K={\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
, there are
\mathbb{C}
-linearly independent units
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}
of
{\operatorname{U}}_{{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]}
with
A(y_{1})=\cdots=A(y_{r})=0
.

Next we describe another incarnation of
{\operatorname{U}}_{{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]}
, namely as a ring
\mathbb{O}
of “oscillating” transseries. Towards this goal we first note that by
[ADH, 11.5.1, 11.8.2] we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{I}({\mathbb{T}})\

\displaystyle=\ \big\{y\in{\mathbb{T}}:\ \text{$y\preccurlyeq f^{\prime}$ for some $f\prec 1$ in ${\mathbb{T}}$}\big\}

\displaystyle=\ \big\{y\in{\mathbb{T}}:\ \text{$y\prec 1/(\ell_{0}\cdots\ell_{n})$ for all $n$}\big\},

so a complement
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}
of
\operatorname{I}({\mathbb{T}})
in
{\mathbb{T}}
is given by

\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}\ :=\ \big\{y\in{\mathbb{T}}:\ \text{$\operatorname{supp}(y)\succ 1/(\ell_{0}\cdots\ell_{n-1}\ell_{n}^{2})$ for all $n$}\big\}.

Since
{\mathbb{T}}^{\dagger}={\mathbb{T}}
and
\operatorname{I}\!\big({\mathbb{T}}[\imag]\big)\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]^{\dagger}
we
have
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]^{\dagger}={\mathbb{T}}\oplus\operatorname{I}({\mathbb{T}})\imag
by Lemmas
I.2.4
and
I.2.13
.
We now take
\Lambda=\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}\imag
as our complement
\Lambda
of
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]^{\dagger}
in
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
and
explain how the universal exponential extension
{\operatorname{U}}
of
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
for this
\Lambda
was introduced in
[
15
, Section 7.7]
in a different way.
Let

{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}\ :=\ \{f\in{\mathbb{T}}:\operatorname{supp}f\succ 1\},

and similarly with
\prec
in place of
\succ
; then
{\mathbb{T}}_{\prec}=\smallo_{{\mathbb{T}}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
are

{\mathbb{R}}
-linear subspaces of
{\mathbb{T}}
, and
{\mathbb{T}}
decomposes as an internal direct sum

(II.4.2)

{\mathbb{T}}\ =\ {\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathbb{T}}_{\prec}

of
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear subspaces of
{\mathbb{T}}
.
Let
\operatorname{e}^{\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}}=\{\operatorname{e}^{\imag f}:f\in{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}\}
be a multiplicative copy of the additive group
{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
, with isomorphism
f\mapsto\operatorname{e}^{\imag f}
. Then we have the group ring

\mathbb{O}\ :=\ K\big[\operatorname{e}^{\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}}\big]

of
\operatorname{e}^{\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}}
over
K={\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
.
We make
\mathbb{O}
into a differential ring extension of
K
by

(\operatorname{e}^{\imag f})^{\prime}\ =\ \imag f^{\prime}\operatorname{e}^{\imag f}\qquad(f\in{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}).

Hence
\mathbb{O}
is an exponential extension of
K
.
The elements of
\mathbb{O}
are called
oscillating transseries.

For each
f\in{\mathbb{T}}
there is a unique
g\in{\mathbb{T}}
, to be denoted by
\int f
,
such that
g^{\prime}=f
and
g
has constant term
g_{1}=0
.
The injective map
\int\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
is
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear; we use this map
to show that
{\operatorname{U}}
and
\mathbb{O}
are disguised versions of each other:

Proposition II.4.21
.

There is a unique isomorphism
{\operatorname{U}}=K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big]\to\mathbb{O}
of differential
K
-algebras sending
\operatorname{e}(h\imag)
to
\operatorname{e}^{\imag\int h}
for all
h\in\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}
.

This requires the next lemma. We assume familiarity with [ADH, Appendix A], especially with the ordered group
G^{\operatorname{LE}}
(a subgroup of
{\mathbb{T}}^{\times}
) of
logarithmic-exponential monomials and its subgroup

G^{\operatorname{E}}=\bigcup_{n}G_{n}
of exponential monomials.

Lemma II.4.22
.

If
{\mathfrak{m}}\in G^{\operatorname{LE}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\succ 1
, then
\operatorname{supp}{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}\ \subseteq\ \Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}
.

Proof.

We first prove by induction on
n
a fact about elements of
G^{\operatorname{E}}
:

\text{ if ${\mathfrak{m}}\in G_{n}$, ${\mathfrak{m}}\succ 1$,
then $\operatorname{supp}{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}\succ 1/x$}.

For
r\in{\mathbb{R}}^{>}
we have
(x^{r})^{\prime}=rx^{r-1}\succ 1/x
,
so the claim holds for
n=0
. Suppose the claim holds for a certain
n
. Now

G_{n+1}=G_{n}\exp(A_{n})
,
G_{n}
is a convex subgroup of
G_{n+1}
, and

A_{n}\ =\ \big\{f\in{\mathbb{R}}[[G_{n}]]:\ \operatorname{supp}f\succ G_{n-1}\big\}\qquad\text{ (where $G_{-1}:=\{1\}$).}

Let
{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathfrak{n}}\exp(a)\in G_{n+1}
where
{\mathfrak{n}}\in G_{n}
,
a\in A_{n}
; then

{\mathfrak{m}}\succ 1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\text{$a>0$, or $a=0$, ${\mathfrak{n}}\succ 1$.}

Suppose
{\mathfrak{m}}\succ 1
. If
a=0
, then
{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathfrak{n}}
, and we are done by inductive hypothesis,
so assume
a>0
. Then
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}+{\mathfrak{n}}a^{\prime})\exp(a)
and

({\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}+{\mathfrak{n}}a^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}[[G_{n}]]
, a differential subfield of
{\mathbb{T}}
, and

\exp(a)>{\mathbb{R}}[[G_{n}]]
, hence
\operatorname{supp}{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}\succ 1\succ 1/x
as required.

Next, suppose
{\mathfrak{m}}\in G^{\operatorname{LE}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\succ 1
. Take
n\geqslant 1
such that
{\mathfrak{m}}{\uparrow}^{n}\in G^{\operatorname{E}}
.
We have
({\mathfrak{m}}{\uparrow}^{n})^{\prime}=({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}\cdot\ell_{0}\ell_{1}\cdots\ell_{n-1}){\uparrow}^{n}
. For
{\mathfrak{n}}\in\operatorname{supp}{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
and using
{\mathfrak{m}}{\uparrow}^{n}\succ 1

this gives

({\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\ell_{0}\ell_{1}\cdots\ell_{n-1}){\uparrow}^{n}\ \succ\ 1/x

by what we proved for monomials in
G^{\operatorname{E}}
. Applying
{\downarrow}_{n}
this yields

{\mathfrak{n}}\succ 1/(\ell_{0}\ell_{1}\cdots\ell_{n})
,
hence
{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}
as claimed.
∎

Proof of Proposition
II.4.21
.

Applying
\der
to the decomposition (
II.4.2
) gives

{\mathbb{T}}\ =\ \der({\mathbb{T}}_{\succ})\oplus\der({\mathbb{T}}_{\prec}).

Now
\der({\mathbb{T}}_{\succ})\subseteq\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}
by Lemma
II.4.22
, and
\der({\mathbb{T}}_{\prec})\subseteq\operatorname{I}({\mathbb{T}})
, and so these two inclusions are equalities. Thus
\int\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}={\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
, from which the proposition follows.
∎

Proposition II.4.23
.

There is a unique group morphism
\exp\colon K={\mathbb{T}}[\imag]\to\mathbb{O}^{\times}

that extends the given exponential maps
\exp\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{\times}
and

\exp\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and such that
\exp(\imag f)=\operatorname{e}^{\imag f}
for all
f\in{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
and
\exp(\varepsilon)=\sum_{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{n!}
for all
\varepsilon\in\smallo
. It is surjective, has kernel
2\pi\imag{\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, and satisfies
\exp(f)^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\exp(f)
for all
f\in K
.

Proof.

The first statement follows easily from the decompositions

K\ =\ {\mathbb{T}}\oplus\imag{\mathbb{T}}\ =\ {\mathbb{T}}\oplus\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}\oplus\imag{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\imag\smallo_{{\mathbb{T}}},\qquad\mathbb{C}\ =\ {\mathbb{R}}\oplus\imag{\mathbb{R}},\qquad\smallo\ =\ \smallo_{{\mathbb{T}}}\oplus\imag\smallo_{{\mathbb{T}}}

of
K
,
\mathbb{C}
, and
\smallo=\smallo_{K}
as internal direct sums of
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear subspaces. Next,

\mathbb{O}^{\times}\ =\ K^{\times}\operatorname{e}^{\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}}=\ {\mathbb{T}}^{>}\cdot S_{\mathbb{C}}\cdot(1+\smallo)\cdot\operatorname{e}^{\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}},\qquad S_{\mathbb{C}}\ :=\ \big\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ |z|=1\big\},

by Lemmas
II.1.1
and
I.2.4
, and Corollary
I.2.7
.
Now
{\mathbb{T}}^{>}=\exp({\mathbb{T}})
and
S_{\mathbb{C}}=\exp(\imag{\mathbb{R}})
, so surjectivity follows from

\exp(\smallo)=1+\smallo
, a consequence of
the well-known bijectivity of the map
\varepsilon\mapsto\sum_{n}\frac{\varepsilon^{n}}{n!}\colon\smallo\to 1+\smallo
, whose inverse is given by

1+\delta\mapsto\log(1+\delta):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\delta^{n}\qquad(\delta\in\smallo).

That the kernel is
2\pi\imag{\mathbb{Z}}
follows from the initial decomposition of the additive group of
K
as
{\mathbb{T}}\oplus\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}\oplus\imag{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\imag\smallo_{{\mathbb{T}}}
. The identity
\exp(f)^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\exp(f)
for
f\in K
follows from it being satisfied for
f\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
f\in\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
,

f\in\mathbb{C}
, and
f\in\smallo
.
∎

To integrate oscillating transseries, note first that the

{\mathbb{R}}
-linear operator
\int\colon{\mathbb{T}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
extends uniquely to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
\int\colon{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]\to{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
. This in turn extends uniquely to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
\int\colon\mathbb{O}\to\mathbb{O}
such that
(\int\Phi)^{\prime}=\Phi
for all
\Phi\in\mathbb{O}
and

\int{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]\operatorname{e}^{\phi\imag}\subseteq{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]\operatorname{e}^{\phi\imag}
for all
\phi\in{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
: given
\phi\in{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}^{\neq}
and
{g\in{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]}
, there is a unique

f\in{\mathbb{T}}[\imag[
such that
(f\operatorname{e}^{\phi\imag})^{\prime}=g\operatorname{e}^{\phi\imag}
: existence holds because
{y^{\prime}+y\phi^{\prime}\imag=g}
has a solution in
{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
, the latter being linearly surjective, and uniqueness holds by Lemma
I.2.3
applied to
K=L={\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
, because
\phi^{\prime}\imag\notin{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]^{\dagger}
in view of remarks preceding Lemma
I.2.13
.

The operator
\int
is a right-inverse of
the linear differential operator
\der
on
\mathbb{O}
. To extend this to other linear differential operators, make the subgroup
G^{\mathbb{O}}:=G^{\operatorname{LE}}\operatorname{e}^{\imag{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}}
of
\mathbb{O}^{\times}
into an ordered group so that the ordered subgroup
G^{\operatorname{LE}}
of
{\mathbb{T}}^{>}
is a convex ordered subgroup of
G^{\mathbb{O}}
and

\operatorname{e}^{\imag\phi}\succ G^{\operatorname{LE}}
for
\phi>0
in
{\mathbb{T}}_{\succ}
. (Possible in only one way.) Next, extend the natural inclusion

{\mathbb{T}}[\imag]\to{\mathbb{C}}[[G^{\operatorname{LE}}]]
to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra embedding
{\mathbb{O}\to{\mathbb{C}}[[G^{\mathbb{O}}]]}
by sending

\operatorname{e}^{\imag\phi}\in\mathbb{O}
to
\operatorname{e}^{\imag\phi}\in G^{\mathbb{O}}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}[[G^{\mathbb{O}}]]
. Identify
\mathbb{O}

with a subalgebra of
{\mathbb{C}}[[G^{\mathbb{O}}]]
via this embedding, so
\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq G^{\mathbb{O}}
for
f\in\mathbb{O}
. It makes the Hahn space
{\mathbb{C}}[[G^{\mathbb{O}}]]
over
{\mathbb{C}}
an immediate extension of its valued subspace
\mathbb{O}
. The latter is in particular also a Hahn space over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
A\in{\mathbb{T}}[\imag][\der]^{\neq}
. Then
A(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{O}
by Lemmas
II.4.3
,
II.4.19
, and Proposition
II.4.21
.
The proof of [ADH, 2.3.22] now gives for each

g\in\mathbb{O}
a unique element
{f=:A^{-1}(g)\in\mathbb{O}}
with
A(f)=g
and

\operatorname{supp}(f)\cap{\mathfrak{d}}\big(\!\ker_{\mathbb{O}}^{\neq}A\big)=\emptyset
.
This requirement on
\operatorname{supp}A^{-1}(g)
yields a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear operator
A^{-1}
on
\mathbb{O}
with
A\circ A^{-1}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{O}}
; we call it the
distinguished
right-inverse of the operator
A
on
\mathbb{O}
.
With this definition
\der^{-1}
is the operator
\int
on
\mathbb{O}
specified earlier.

In the next section we explore various valuations on universal exponential extensions (such as
\mathbb{O}
)
with additional properties.

II.5.
Valuations on the Universal Exponential Extension

In this section
K
is a valued differential field with algebraically closed constant field
{C\subseteq\mathcal{O}}
and divisible group
K^{\dagger}
of
logarithmic derivatives.

Then
\Gamma=v(K^{\times})
is also divisible,
since we have a group isomorphism

va\mapsto a^{\dagger}+(\mathcal{O}^{\times})^{\dagger}\ :\ \Gamma\to K^{\dagger}/(\mathcal{O}^{\times})^{\dagger}\qquad(a\in K^{\times}).

Let
\Lambda
be a complement of the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspace
K^{\dagger}
of
K
, let
\lambda
range over
\Lambda
,
let
{{\operatorname{U}}=K\big[\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big]}
be the universal exponential extension of
K
constructed in Section
II.2

and set
\Omega:=\operatorname{Frac}({\operatorname{U}})
. Thus
\Omega
is a differential field with
constant field
C
.

The gaussian extension

We equip
{\operatorname{U}}

with the gaussian extension
v_{\operatorname{g}}
of the valuation of
K
as defined in Section
II.1
; so for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
with spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda})
:

v_{\operatorname{g}}(f)\ =\ \min_{\lambda}v(f_{\lambda}),

and hence

v_{\operatorname{g}}(f^{\prime})\ =\ \min_{\lambda}v(f_{\lambda}^{\prime}+\lambda f_{\lambda}).

The field
\Omega
with the valuation extending
v_{\operatorname{g}}
is a valued differential field extension of
K
, but it can happen that

K
has small derivation, whereas
\Omega
does not:

Example
.

Let
K=C(\!(t^{{\mathbb{Q}}})\!)
and
\Lambda
be as in Example
II.2.4
, so
t\prec 1\prec x=t^{-1}
and
t^{\prime}=-t^{2}
. Then
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued of
H
-type with small derivation, but in
\Omega
with the above valuation,

t\operatorname{e}(x)\ \prec\ 1,\qquad\big(t\operatorname{e}(x)\big){}^{\prime}\ =\ -t^{2}\operatorname{e}(x)+\operatorname{e}(x)\ \sim\ \operatorname{e}(x)\ \asymp\ 1.

To obtain an example where
K=H[\imag]
for a Liouville closed
H
-field
H
and
\imag^{2}=-1
, take
K:={\mathbb{T}}[\imag]
and
\Lambda:=\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}\imag
as at the end of Section
II.4
. Now
x\in\Lambda_{{\mathbb{T}}}
and in
\Omega
equipped with the above valuation we have for
t:=x^{-1}
:

t\operatorname{e}(x\imag)\ \prec\ 1,\qquad\big(t\operatorname{e}(x\imag)\big){}^{\prime}\ =\ -t^{2}\operatorname{e}(x\imag)+\imag\operatorname{e}(x\imag)\ \sim\ \imag\operatorname{e}(x\imag)\ \asymp\ 1,

so
\big(t\operatorname{e}(x\imag)\big){}^{\prime}\not\prec t^{\dagger}
, hence
\Omega
is neither asymptotic nor has small derivation.

However, we show next that under certain assumptions on
K
with small derivation,
\Omega
has also a valuation
which does make
\Omega
a valued differential field extension of
K
with small derivation.
For this we rely on results from [ADH, 10.4].
Although such a valuation is less canonical than
v_{\operatorname{g}}
, it is useful for harnessing the finiteness statements about the set
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A)
of eventual exceptional values of
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}

from Section
I.4
to obtain similar facts about the set of
ultimate exceptional values
of
A
introduced later in this section.

Spectral extensions

In this subsection
K
is
\operatorname{d}
-valued of
H
-type with
\Gamma\neq\{0\}
and with small derivation.

Lemma II.5.1
.

The valuation of
K
extends to a valuation on the field
\Omega
that makes
\Omega
a
\operatorname{d}
-valued extension of
K
of
H
-type with small derivation.

Proof.

Applying [ADH, 10.4.7] to an algebraic closure of
K
gives a
\operatorname{d}
-valued algebraically closed extension
L
of
K
of
H
-type with small derivation and
C_{L}=C

such that
L^{\dagger}\supseteq K
.
Let
E:=\{y\in L^{\times}:\,y^{\dagger}\in K\}
, so
E
is a subgroup of
L^{\times}
,
E^{\dagger}=K
, and
K[E]
is an exponential extension of
K
with
C_{K[E]}=C
. Then Corollary
II.2.10

gives an embedding
{{\operatorname{U}}\to L}
of differential
K
-algebras with image
K[E]
, which extends to an
embedding
\Omega\to L
of differential fields. Using this embedding to transfer the valuation of
L
to
\Omega
gives a valuation as required.
∎

A
spectral extension
of the valuation of
K
to
\Omega
is a valuation on the field
\Omega
with the properties stated in Lemma
II.5.1
.
If
K
is
\upomega
-free, then so is
\Omega
equipped with any spectral extension of the valuation of
K
,
by [ADH, 13.6] (and then
\Omega
has rational asymptotic integration by [ADH, 11.7]).
We do not know whether this goes through with “
\uplambda
-free” instead of “
\upomega
-free”. Here is something weaker:

Lemma II.5.2
.

Suppose
K
is algebraically closed and
\uplambda
-free. Then some spectral extension of the valuation of
K
to
\Omega
makes

\Omega
a
\operatorname{d}
-valued field with divisible value group and asymptotic integration.

Proof.

Take
L
,
E
and an embedding
\Omega\to L
as in the proof of Lemma
II.5.1
. Use this embedding to identify
\Omega
with a differential subfield of
L
, so
U=K[E]
and
\Omega=K(E)
, and
equip
\Omega
with the spectral extension of the valuation of
K
obtained by restricting the valuation of
L
to
\Omega
.
Since
L
is algebraically closed,
E
is divisible, and
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma+v(E)
by [ADH, 10.4.7(iv)]. So
\Gamma_{\Omega}=\Gamma_{L}
is divisible.
Let
a\in K^{\times}
,
y\in E
. Then
K(y)

has asymptotic integration by Proposition
I.3.12
, hence
v(ay)\in(\Gamma_{K(y)}^{\neq})^{\prime}\subseteq(\Gamma_{\Omega}^{\neq})^{\prime}
. Thus
\Omega
has asymptotic integration.
∎

In the rest of this subsection
\Omega
is equipped with a spectral extension
v
(with value group
\Gamma_{\Omega}
) of the valuation of
K
. The proof of Lemma
II.5.1
and [ADH, 10.4.7] show that we can choose
v
so that
\Psi_{\Omega}\subseteq\Gamma
;
but under suitable hypotheses on
K
, this is automatic:

Lemma II.5.3
.

Suppose
K
has asymptotic integration and
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
.
Then
{\Psi_{\Omega}\subseteq\Gamma}
, the group morphism

(II.5.1)

\lambda\mapsto v\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)\ \colon\ \Lambda\to\Gamma_{\Omega}

is injective, and
\Gamma_{\Omega}
is divisible with
\Gamma_{\Omega}=\Gamma\oplus v\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big)

(
internal direct sum of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear subspaces of
\Gamma_{\Omega}
)
. Moreover,

\Psi_{\Omega}=\Psi^{\downarrow}
in
\Gamma
.

Proof.

For
a\in K^{\times}
we have
\big(a\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big){}^{\dagger}=a^{\dagger}+\lambda\in K
, and
if
a\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\asymp 1
, then

a^{\dagger}+\lambda\ =\ \big(a\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big){}^{\dagger}\in(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{\Omega})^{\dagger}\cap K\ \subseteq\ \operatorname{I}(\Omega)\cap K\ =\ \operatorname{I}(K),

so
\lambda\in\Lambda\cap\big(\operatorname{I}(K)+K^{\dagger}\big)=\Lambda\cap K^{\dagger}=\{0\}
and
a\asymp 1
. Thus for
a_{1},a_{2}\in K^{\times}
and
distinct
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\Lambda
we have
a_{1}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{1})\not\asymp a_{2}\operatorname{e}(\lambda_{2})
, and so
for
f\in{\operatorname{U}}
with spectral decomposition
(f_{\lambda})
we have

vf=\min_{\lambda}v\big(f_{\lambda}\operatorname{e}(\lambda)\big)
. Hence

\Psi_{\Omega}\ \subseteq\big\{v(a^{\dagger}+\lambda):\ a\in K^{\times},\ \lambda\in\Lambda\big\}\ =\ v(K)\ =\ \Gamma_{\infty},

the map (
II.5.1
) is injective and

\Gamma\cap v\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big)=\{0\}
, and so

\Gamma_{\Omega}=\Gamma\oplus v\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big)
(internal direct sum of subgroups
of
\Gamma_{\Omega}
). Since
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are divisible, so is
\Gamma_{\Omega}
.
Now
\Psi_{\Omega}=\Psi^{\downarrow}
follows from
K=({\operatorname{U}}^{\times})^{\dagger}\subseteq\Omega^{\dagger}

and
K
having asymptotic integration.
∎

We can now improve on Lemma
II.4.1
:

Corollary II.5.4
.

Suppose
K
has asymptotic integration and
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
, and let
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
. Then the
C
-linear space
\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A
has a basis

\mathcal{B}\subseteq\operatorname{U}^{\times}
such that
v
is injective on
\mathcal{B}
and

v(\mathcal{B})=v(\ker^{\neq}_{\operatorname{U}}A)
, and thus
\lvert v(\ker^{\neq}_{\operatorname{U}}A)\rvert=\dim_{C}\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A
.

Proof.

By [ADH, 5.6.6] we have a basis
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
of the
C
-linear space
\ker_{K}A_{\lambda}
such that
v
is injective on
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
and
v(\mathcal{B}_{\lambda})=v(\ker^{\neq}_{K}A_{\lambda})
.
Then
\mathcal{B}:=\bigcup_{\lambda}\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\operatorname{e}(\lambda)
is a basis of
\ker_{\operatorname{U}}A
. It has the desired properties by Lemma
II.5.3
.
∎

Corollary II.5.5
.

Suppose
K
is
\uplambda
-free and
\operatorname{I}(K)\subseteq K^{\dagger}
. Then
\Omega
has asymptotic integration, and so its
H
-asymptotic couple is closed by Lemma
II.5.3
.

Proof.

By Lemma
II.5.3
,
\Gamma_{\Omega}=\Gamma+v\big(\!\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)\big)
. Using Proposition
I.3.12
as in the proof of Lemma
II.5.2
, with
\operatorname{e}(\Lambda)
in place of
E
, shows
\Omega
has asymptotic integration.
∎

Ultimate exceptional values

In this subsection
K
is
H
-asymptotic with small derivation and asymptotic integration.

Also
A\in K[\der]^{\neq}
and
r:=\operatorname{order}(A)
, and
\gamma
ranges over
\Gamma=v(K^{\times})
.
We have
v(\ker^{\neq}A_{\lambda})\subseteq{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A_{\lambda})
, so if

\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
A
with respect to
\lambda
, then
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A_{\lambda})\neq\emptyset
.
We call the elements of the set

{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}(A)\ =\ {\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}_{K}(A)\ :=\ \bigcup_{\lambda}\,{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{e}}(A_{\lambda})\ =\ \big\{\gamma:\,\text{$\operatorname{nwt}_{A_{\lambda}}(\gamma)\geqslant 1$ for some $\lambda$}\big\}

the
ultimate exceptional values of
A
with respect to
\Lambda
. The definition of
{\mathscr{E}}^{\operatorname{u}}_{K}(A)
involves our choice of
\Lambda
